Beyond adding places, these make a state better. Do them when asked, or note in section 10 that they are still open.

| Step | When | What to do |
|---|---|---|
| Law note | **Optional. Only when the owner asks** | Add the state's trespass basics to the in-app Guide and set `legal` to `True` in `states.py`. Read the statute; do not paraphrase from memory. A batch is complete without it: the owner's position is that knowing the local law is up to whoever visits. What is never optional is the access line on each place (who owns it, whether it is open, closed or posted) |
| State imagery | When the state publishes it | Add its leaf-off and LiDAR services to `leafUrl()` and `lidarUrl()` in `app.js`, the aerial to `SRC` in `tools/ortho.py` (the pin check and the LiDAR sheets read it), its credit to `CREDIT` in `app.js`, and its name to the note under Map options and the Guide tab's credits. Before wiring one in: (1) ask it for eight tiles at once and time them, at two zooms; (2) check it sends CORS headers to a page on another site; (3) note its deepest zoom; (4) look at a frame, and say "leaf-off" only if the publisher does or you say what you saw; (5) ask for a square outside its coverage and one over open water. Ready-cut tiles (`.../tile/{z}/{y}/{x}`) go in with `xyz()` from zoom 12. A cut-to-order image service goes in with `exportUrl(..., 'jpgpng')` from the zoom where it answers in a second or two: with `jpgpng` it sends a see-through PNG where it has no data and the app lays the world's tile beneath. A map service (`/MapServer/export`) cannot do that; name the picture layer alone (`&layers=show:N`), because a mosaic's group layer also draws tile outlines (Pennsylvania's did, as bright green lines). A server that takes more than about three seconds a tile goes in `SLOWHOST` so it is given time. The thresholds test `z`, the map's zoom, never `c.z` (section 9). Then add a line to `tools/tiles_test.js` (part 8) and run it, and look at the real thing with a short Playwright script through the proxy, phone size and wide. New Hampshire's first candidate, a map service, returned blank white squares at random under load; its ready-cut tile service did not. Maine's state sign-in service was the wrong door: the public one is under `gis.maine.gov/image/`. Utah's needs a key and was left out. |
| Rail and canal lines | When the owner asks for a state | See "Lines and leads for a new state" below. Done for New York, the six New England states, New Jersey and Pennsylvania |
| OpenStreetMap leads (mapped ruins) | Done for every state, the District of Columbia and the ten provinces | See "Leads for every state and province" below, which also says what is left out on purpose |
| Owner lookup and lot lines | Can wait | Add to `lookup()` and `drawLots()` if the state publishes parcels with owner names |
| A new region or start city | When a state's regions are too coarse, or its one statewide region holds more than a few dozen places | Add it in `states.py`: for example `('txw','West Texas')` beside the statewide `('tx','Texas')`. Leave the statewide code in place for the places already using it; never rename a region code that published places use. Check a new start city's coordinate with a geocoder, not from memory |

### Starting a new chat from nothing

A new session knows nothing about this project. With the repository attached, this prompt is enough to go from an empty area to documented pins in a pull request:

```
Read CLAUDE.md and GUIDE.md in the Lost-and-Hidden-Atlas repository, then add a batch of places for
<city, county or region, and state>. Follow GUIDE.md sections 3 to 6 exactly: build the workspace with
setup.sh, find candidates from list pages, read a source for every fact, write one data_<area>.py batch,
look at every pin with pincheck.py and say in each pin note what the aerial showed, cut old maps, run
all.sh, check.py and the smoke test, add what you could not source to LEADS.md, and open a pull request
that says what you verified and what you could not. Do not push to main or merge. Aim for <N> places,
ruins and traces first.
```

Name the area and a number; everything else is in the guide. If the area is a city the Atlas has no start city for, the session should add one to `tools/states.py` (the table above).

### Lines and leads for a new state

The old rail, trolley and canal lines exist for nine states: New York and Massachusetts, and since November 17, 2026 Connecticut, Rhode Island, Vermont, New Hampshire, Maine, New Jersey and Pennsylvania. Every other state has none yet. (The leads layer no longer waits for the lines: since November 19, 2026 it covers every state and province, by the lighter route under "Leads for every state and province" below. These steps still download a state's leads along with its lines, and in the nine states those are the leads on the map.) To add a state's lines (its outline must already be in `build/states.json`):

1. `python3 tools/osm_layers.py <key>` downloads the state's abandoned, disused and razed railways, its canals, and its mapped ruins from the OpenStreetMap layers Esri hosts. It looks each old railway up in the highways layer to tell which are rail trails today. About ten minutes for New York.
2. `python3 tools/ohm_state.py <key>` downloads OpenHistoricalMap's railways and canals, which carry opening and closing years.
3. Add the key to `STATE_KEYS` at the top of **both** `tools/lines.py` and `tools/leads.py`.
4. `python3 tools/lines.py` (about two minutes with all nine states; it prints where the time and memory go) and `python3 tools/leads.py`, then `all.sh`. Both keep the existing lines and leads for every area whose raw files you do not have, so a fresh workspace only rebuilds the new state. Railroads in use come from the federal rail network and are fetched for the new state by `lines.py` itself. `lines.py` files every piece of line under the state its middle is in (`s`); `all.sh` then cuts the per-state files the hosted page fetches, and `python3 tools/lines_split.py` shows how big each came out.
5. Look at the result at phone size over the state's cities, and tap a few lines. Two things are told by name, and both lists are in `lines.py`. Trolley and interurban lines (`TROL`): add the state's interurban company names if they show as plain abandoned railroad, each checked in a source, and look at what the pattern caught that is not a trolley line (a works siding named for General Electric was one). Canals outside New York (`OLDCANAL`): the layers give only a name, so a towpath canal must be listed to show as an old canal; any other named canal shows as in use. Canals still in use in New York are listed by name in the same file.
6. Run `tools/lines_test.js`, and say in the pull request how many kilometres of each kind were added (`lines.py` prints them state by state).

What the layer route loses compared with a full OpenStreetMap download: a line tagged only as `abandoned:railway=…` with no `railway=abandoned` is missing (about 5% by length in the Capital Region, where both were available to compare), and old names kept in tags such as `old_name` are missing, so more lines are unnamed. Leads from the layers are thinner than from Overpass: only mapped historic points and ruined or abandoned buildings.

The downloads of neighbouring states overlap, because each is a box round its state. `lines.py` therefore takes a way once, keeps all of it that lies in any state being rebuilt, and prefers the copy from the first Overpass downloads where there is one. One gap is known and left: a railway mapped as a route relation comes from the layers as several pieces under one id and only the first is used (the comment in `lines.py` has the numbers).

**Leads for one metropolitan area, without the rail lines.** `python3 tools/osm_leads_box.py --skip-mines <west> <south> <east> <north> <key> [<key>]` downloads only the leads for a box and writes `cache/osm/<key>_leads.json` for each state the box touches; list the keys in `STATE_KEYS` in `tools/leads.py` (not `lines.py`) and run it. This was done for Kansas City in November 2026 (box -95.80 38.20 -93.40 40.00, keys `mo` and `ks`): 26 leads. `--skip-mines` leaves out mapped mine entrances, which round Kansas City are nearly all the doors of working underground business parks. The old rail and interurban lines for Missouri and Kansas were not added: `data/lines.json` is one 2.3 MB file loaded at start, and two more big rail states would need it split by state first.

**Leads for every state and province (November 19, 2026).** `python3 tools/osm_leads_box.py --skip-mines --state <key> [<key> ...]` downloads one state or province at a time, each with the box of its own outline, into `cache/osm/<key>_leads.json` (five seconds to a minute and a half each; Ontario seven minutes; a key whose file is there is skipped). Every key is already in `STATE_KEYS` in `tools/leads.py` (`FIRST`, the ten states done with their lines or by a box, and `MORE`, the other 39 states, the District of Columbia and the ten provinces). `python3 tools/tribal.py` (a minute, once) then `python3 tools/leads.py` rebuilds the states whose files are present and keeps every other lead as it is, so refreshing one state needs only that state's download. The layer went from 1,963 leads to 7,405: 1,962 in the first eleven states, 4,631 in the other states and the District, 812 in Canada; 3,191 have a name. It is one file for nearly everybody, `data/leads.json`, 253 KB, fetched when Leads is switched on or the Near tab wants leads (a second file holds a category that is off by default: below); if it passes about 500 KB, cut it by state the way the vanished places and mines are (below) before adding more.

What `leads.py` does differently for the states in `MORE`, and why (the numbers are from the first run):
- **Native American sites and leads on reservation land are a category of their own, which a reader must switch on** (the owner's decision, November 19, 2026: on the map, but not by default). Outside the Northeast most of what OpenStreetMap calls an archaeological site is a mound, a pueblo, rock art or a heiau (1,395 of Colorado's 1,822 raw leads were inside Mesa Verde), and many are sacred, protected by law or closed. They are kept out of `data/leads.json` and written to a second file, `build/leads_native.json`, which the site carries as `data/leads/native-<stamp>.json`; `data/leads.json` names it in `x.native` (`n` rows, stamp `h`). 2,370 rows after thinning, 81 KB. What goes there, and under which kind: everything tagged `historic=archaeological_site` ("Archaeological site", 2,108; a few are a homestead or a fossil dig, which is why the kind says no more than the tag does); any other lead whose name says so ("Native American site", `NATIVE` in `leads.py`, 52); any other lead on reservation or trust land ("Mapped ruin on reservation land" and the like, 88; the Census Bureau's outlines, fetched by `tools/tribal.py` into `cache/tribal.json`, and `leads.py` stops if that file is missing); and "Probably a Native American site" (122): every other lead in the `FOURCORNERS` box, which takes in Cedar Mesa, Hovenweep, Mesa Verde and the Mancos valley, where a mapped "ruin" is nearly always an ancestral Puebloan one whatever it is called, and any lead within 1.5 km of three or more of the first two kinds. The rules do not catch every one: an unnamed ruin on public land in New Mexico or Arizona can be such a site and sit in the main file, and Canada's reserves are not in the outlines. The first eleven states keep their 122 "Archaeological site" leads in the main file as before.
  - **In the app** (`leadsMore()`, `leadRows()`, the setting `nativeLeads`, off in `SET0`): Settings, under Map, "Show Native American sites among the leads". Off, the second file is not fetched and nothing of it is drawn, listed in Near or buzzed. On, it is fetched once (and kept by the service worker, or by the Android app, which does not carry it built in), its rows are turquoise dots beside the yellow ones while Unchecked leads is on, and a tapped one carries a line saying to go only where whoever looks after the place invites visitors. **Never fold these rows into `data/leads.json`:** the Android app fetches that file from the site while still running the `app.js` it was built with, so an older copy of the app would show them to everyone. `tools/overlay_test.js` checks all of it (twelve checks at Mesa Verde).
  - **For research:** `tools/start.py` gives the number of such leads near the ground and does not list them. **A session writes one up only where whoever manages it invites visitors and says so on its own page** (a national monument, a state historic site, a tribal park's tours), like any other place whose access must be stated; otherwise it leaves it and says so in its note.
- **Mapped mine entrances** (`--skip-mines`): the mines layer has the USGS records for every state, and in the West OpenStreetMap's are mostly the same records.
- **Clumps of unnamed ruins: 1,905.** An unnamed lead within 150 m (`CLUMP`) of another unnamed lead of the same kind is dropped, so a ghost town or a burnt street is a few dots and not fifty. The war bases at Dutch Harbor and Cold Bay in Alaska were 1,394 mapped hut sites. Named leads are all kept. This applies to any state that is rebuilt, the first ten included; they were not rebuilt in this run and kept every lead they had.

A lead within 150 m of an Atlas place is dropped as before, so the count falls a little as places are added. **Checked:** every lead on the map before is still there except Fort Mott in New Jersey, which became a place on November 18; no row is in both files; running `leads.py` twice gives the same two files; no name in the new leads holds a telephone number, an e-mail address or anything about a living person that a scan for those could find. **Not checked:** the leads themselves. Nobody looked at an aerial for any of them, and a name such as "Abandoned house" is one mapper's word.

Lead dots show from zoom 6 in (`LEADZ` in `app.js`). With the whole continent covered, a view of half the country held 6,600 dots and a zoom step took about half a second on the slowed phone profile (`tools/zoom_prof.js`); at zoom 6 the most a view holds is about what the Northeast alone held before. Switching Leads on in a wider view says "Zoom in to see them". `tools/overlay_test.js` checks that Texas has dots, that zooms 4 and 5 have none and that zoom 6 has them back.

**Publishing a leads rebuild:** `LAYERS=1 bash tools/publish.sh …` carries `data/leads.json` and `data/leads/`, and with them whatever the workspace built for the other layers. After it, put back every other layer file from `origin/main` unless you rebuilt that layer too (`git status` shows them; `git restore --staged --worktree --source=origin/main -- data/lines.json …`): a workspace's raw files for the lines are older than what is published.

### Historic pockets for a new state

The owner's idea: showing every historical plaque is clutter, and most plaques mark something that is gone, but where many sit close together, with listed buildings among them, there is usually an old town centre worth walking ("some of my best finds as far as buildings have been just walking through old towns"). The layer ("Historic pockets" in Map options) therefore puts **old town centres** first:

- An **old town centre** is one purple disc per town, carrying the town's name, ranked by a score. The best ones in view are labelled, and Map options lists the ones within 40 miles of the start place (of the middle of the map, for a reader who has no start place yet), richest or nearest first.
- Every **other pocket** (an estate, a mill site, a battlefield, a campus, a city neighbourhood away from the centre) is a dashed grey ring. "Old town centres only" in Map options hides them.
- The single markers and listings appear only from zoom 15. Zoomed out, only the richest discs show, so a state full of old villages is not one purple smear (`pockFrom` in `app.js`).
- **What is drawn, since November 19, 2026 (the owner's notes after using it round Albany).** No disc is drawn wider than the 20-mile scale (`POCKZ`, zoom 8): out there the grouped pins already say where the places are. At street level the **listed buildings** (white dots, the National Register) show by default and the **historical markers** (purple dots, HMdb.org) only when switched on: each has its own switch under Historic pockets in Map options (`U.ui.pockR`, on unless `false`; `U.ui.pockM`, off unless set). The dashed outlines round historic districts are no longer drawn: a box round half a city told nobody anything. The district data still travels in the detail files, in case a use is found for it. The layer bar has a button for it, "Old towns" (`pock` in `BAR`), so it can be switched off without opening Map options. `tools/overlay_test.js` checks all of this in downtown Albany.
- **Quieter still, November 20, 2026** (the owner, with every state in: "it pops up this box that takes up the whole screen"). (1) The **other pockets** (the dashed rings: estates, campuses, city neighbourhoods) are off until "Also show other pockets" is ticked (`U.ui.pockO`; it replaces "Old town centres only"). (2) A **weak town centre beside a stronger one** is left off the map: score under 15 with a stronger centre within 3 km (`pockThin()`, `k.thin`); a weak one with nothing near it stays, and the list in Map options and the Near tab keep them all. About 480 of the 7,649 centres are thinned. (3) A **big disc is tapped at its middle**: when a disc is more than 32 px in radius only a tap near its centre, where a dot is now drawn, opens its box, so a tap on the lake in the park inside it does nothing. (4) **The box is small**: the name, what it is and its score, the counts, and "Zoom in". The sentences explaining the ranking and the "plaques mostly speak of" line are gone; so are the two map links. The boxes for a single marker or listing lost a sentence each.
- **Research sessions are shown the old town centres** (`tools/nearby.py`, which `start.py` runs): those within reach, the ones with no Atlas place yet first, each with its score, its listings and districts and how many Atlas places lie inside it. A reader taps a purple disc expecting places; this is how sessions are sent where there are none yet.

Since November 20, 2026 the layer covers every state and the District of Columbia: 17,929 pockets, 7,649 of them old town centres, from the marker lists of all fifty states and the National Register. Canada is not covered: the Register is American, and the provinces would need their own list of listed buildings. The raw downloads (Register lists, settlement points, marker lists) are in the maintainer's workspace only; `pockets.py` keeps every state whose raw files it does not have, so one state can be refreshed alone. Where a county's marker list is cut at 500 the pockets there are undercounted, and the District's rests mostly on the Register. Nobody has walked the rankings outside the Northeast.

**How the layer travels (since November 20, 2026; `pockLoad()`, `pockNeed()`, `pockFetch()` in `app.js`, the pockets part of `tools/make.py`).** In one file the layer was 377 KB with nine states and 605 KB with fifteen, on its way to 2 MB, and every reader who switched it on fetched all of it. Now it goes out in pieces, as the vanished places and mines do: `data/pock/index.json` (3 KB: for each state its counts, the stamp `h` of its detail file, the stamp `kh` of its discs and the box `b` its pockets lie in), each state's discs in `data/pock/<state>-k-<kh>.json`, and its markers and listings in `data/pock/<state>.json` as before. The page fetches the index when the layer is switched on, a state's discs when the map looks at it at zoom 8 or closer, when the list in Map options wants the centres within 40 miles, or when the Near tab wants town centres; and the detail at street level. `data/pockets.json`, the whole layer in one file, is what copies of the Android app built before this read, and it fetches every changed state's detail eagerly: it **stays, frozen at the fifteen states it then held** (`PK_OLD` in `make.py`); do not add to it. In a workspace `build/pockets.json` is still the whole layer (`setup.sh` puts the pieces together again). The Android app carries the index and, for the first nine states (`XL_BUILT_IN` in `android-app/scripts/make-www.js`), the discs and detail; any other state's files are fetched when wanted and kept. After new data arrives it refreshes a state's detail only where the phone already has it (`pockSync()` in `boot.js`). Tests: `tools/overlay_test.js` (the town-centres block) and `tools/app_pockets.js`. `LAYERS=1 publish.sh` replaces `data/pock/` whole.

To add a state (about an hour, nearly all of it waiting on step 4):

1. `python3 tools/nrhp_state.py "<STATE NAME>" <key>` downloads the National Register listings from the National Park Service (public domain).
2. `python3 tools/wd_nrhp.py <key>` cuts the same listings out of Wikidata (public domain), which `pockets.py` uses to put the Park Service file right. The first run in a workspace asks a public copy of Wikidata **once** for the whole country and keeps the answer in `cache/wd/nrhp_all.json`. Never put that question in a loop: when this was written Wikidata's own query service was refusing all but one request a minute.
3. `python3 tools/osm_places.py <key>` downloads the state's settlement points (city, town, village, hamlet) from the copy of OpenStreetMap that Esri hosts.
4. `python3 tools/hmdb_state.py "<State Name>" <key>` downloads each county's list of markers from HMdb.org: number, title, town, position, the earliest year the title or first line names (as a number), and whether it is filed as a war memorial. It waits between pages: ten minutes for a small state, forty for New York or Pennsylvania. The county names come from HMdb.org's own page of geographical names, because not every state is filed by county (Connecticut is filed by planning region).
5. Add the key to `KEYS` in `tools/pockets.py`, run it, then `all.sh`. It rebuilds the states whose raw files you have and keeps every other state as it is (`setup.sh` copies the built layer from the repository into the workspace for exactly this).
6. `python3 tools/pock_report.py <lat> <lon> <miles>` prints the ranking round a town you know. Read it: are the top names the places a person would walk for old buildings? Then look at a few towns at phone size with the layer on.

**The rules for HMdb.org data, which are not optional:**

- Its copyright page allows copying its information without asking only for uses that are **not commercial**, and only when it is **credited to HMdb.org with a link**. The app credits and links every marker it shows and names HMdb.org in the Guide. Never store inscriptions or photos. Do not reuse the marker data in anything that earns money without written permission from HMdb.org.
- It shows the first 500 markers of any list without signing in and asks for a sign-in beyond that. **Never sign in and never work around that limit** (for example by cutting a county into towns). A county with more than 500 markers stays incomplete, and `hmdb_state.py` prints which ones. The owner can register (free) and download a full list by hand if a county matters.
- Wait several seconds between pages, as the tool does, and run one state at a time.

**How a pocket is made and ranked** (`tools/pockets.py`; every number below is a named constant at the top of that file):

1. *The listings are put right first.* The Park Service file has three faults that matter here, and Wikidata mends them: a listing the two sources place more than 250 m apart takes Wikidata's position (checked against town points for listings named after a town, Wikidata was the closer about twice in three); a "building" that counts eight or more contributing buildings is a district filed under one of its houses (Hudson's whole downtown is "Robert Jenkins House") and takes Wikidata's name; and a listing the Park Service file lacks is added, without a building count.
2. *Chains.* Markers within 30 m of each other are one spot (a memorial walk with hundreds of plaques counts as a handful of spots). Spots and listings within 220 m of each other chain together.
3. *Old town centres.* A chain that comes within 300 m of a hamlet's point (400 m village, 500 m town, 600 m city) belongs to that settlement, out to 500 m from the point (700, 800, 1100). A district named after the settlement ("Cobleskill Historic District", "Kingston Stockade District") belongs to it up to 2.5 km away (3.5 km for a city), because the Register's coordinate for a district is the middle of a rough box and is often a kilometre off. Everything a settlement gathers is **one** pocket with the settlement's name. It is kept as a town centre only with evidence: a district of eight or more buildings, or listed buildings worth four points, or three plaque spots about things from before 1901 (the exact test is `is_centre`). A district whose own name says Village, Downtown, Main Street, Commercial, Business or Central is a centre even with no settlement point (an old village swallowed by a city, such as Lansingburgh in Troy).
4. *Other pockets.* What is left over, if it weighs enough (a spot 1, a listed place 2, a district 3 to 30). A leftover chain longer than 900 m is cut into 600 m blocks.
5. *The score* (about 56 at most) counts what still stands before what is only remembered: up to 22 for the contributing buildings of districts (4 × log2(1 + buildings ÷ 15), so 45 buildings is 8 and 450 is 20), up to 15 for individually listed buildings (a church, courthouse, bank, hotel, depot, store, mill or hall counts 2, anything else 1), up to 15 for plaque spots (1.5 each for a year up to 1830, 1.2 up to 1900, 0.6 up to 1945, 0.3 later, 0.7 undated, 0.4 for a war memorial), and 4 when a district's name marks a centre.

`python3 tools/pock_panel.py` is the panel the numbers were tuned on: 63 towns round Albany that ought to be centres and ten estates, battlefields and cemeteries that ought not. It needs New York's raw files. When this was written 57 of the 63 were centres under their own name; the other six (Castleton-on-Hudson, Stillwater, New Lebanon, Tivoli, Windham, Waterville) have no Register district and too few plaques for any rule to find. Run it after changing a number.

**The files.** `data/pockets.json` is the index: `k` is one row per pocket, `[lat, lon, radius m, markers, listed places, districts, contributing buildings, name, state, marker spots, type, score, era, settlement kind]` (type `c` = old town centre, `p` = other; rows are sorted centres first, best score first, and `app.js` relies on that), and `s` holds each state's counts and `h`, a stamp of its detail file. `data/pock/<state>.json` is the detail: `m` markers `[lat, lon, HMdb number, title]`, `r` listings `[lat, lon, reference, name, kind, contributing buildings or -1, year listed]`, `d` district outlines. The website fetches a detail file when the map is zoomed to 15 near that state's pockets. The Android app carries the files it was built with; `boot.js` fetches a state's detail from the site when the app does not have it, and after a data update downloads again only the states whose stamp changed. `node source/tools/app_pockets.js` checks those three cases; run it after touching any of this.

### Vanished places and old mines for a new state

Two more layers of unchecked leads, each with its own switch under Leads in Map options. Since November 19, 2026 they cover every state and the District of Columbia: 124,693 vanished places and 118,128 old mines and quarries (they covered eleven states before, with 20,862 and 17,581; the owner asked for more leads for research sessions to work from, in the states that had none). Illinois, Missouri and Ohio have the most vanished places; California, Missouri and Nevada the most mines. The Canadian provinces have neither: both sources are United States files. Since November 19, 2026 each layer goes out in pieces, so that more states can be added without everybody downloading them: `data/<layer>/index.json` (the kinds of point, and for each state its count, a stamp of its file and the box it lies in) and one file per state, `data/<layer>/<state>-<stamp>.json`. `make.py` cuts them from `build/vanished.json` and `build/mines.json`, which stay whole (`start.py` reads those). The app fetches the index when the layer is switched on, and a state's file the first time the map is zoomed in over that state or the reader stands in it (`xlLoad()`, `xlNeed()` in `app.js`; test: `tools/overlay_test.js`). `data/vanished.json` and `data/mines.json`, each whole layer in one file, are still written for copies of the Android app built before that day, which read nothing else; they hold only the eleven states they held then (`XL_OLD` in `make.py`) and can be dropped once those copies are gone. All the states together are 15 MB, so the Android app builds in only the index and the nine Northeast states (`XL_BUILT_IN` in `make-www.js`) and fetches any other state from the Atlas address the first time the map is zoomed in there, keeping it on the phone (`A.fetch` in `boot.js`). **How the layers travel:** like the old lines and the pockets, the built layer lives in the repository's `data/` folder and nowhere else, since the raw downloads are too big to keep. `setup.sh` puts the per-state pieces together again into `build/<layer>.json` (checked: the same rows come back); a pull request that changes a layer carries the pieces with `LAYERS=1 tools/publish.sh`. A resync overwrites `build/vanished.json` and `build/mines.json` with the repository's, so after rebuilding a layer keep a copy until the pull request is merged. `LAYERS=1` also copies `data/leads.json` and the lines: if the workspace built those from older raw files, put the repository's back (`git restore --source=origin/main data/leads.json`) before committing. Both sources are works of the U.S. government and in the public domain. There are thousands of points, so the app draws only the ones in view, from town level in.

- **Vanished places** (`tools/vanished.py`, orange dots). The U.S. Board on Geographic Names marks a feature "(historical)" when it no longer exists or no longer serves its purpose. The file used is the one of August 25, 2021, the last issued before the Board stopped keeping schools, churches, post offices, mines, hospitals and airfields; today's files and map service hold almost none of these. Every state's key is in `KEYS`; to rebuild, download each `<ST>_Features_20210825.txt` (the command is at the top of the tool; 300 MB for all 51), run the tool, then `all.sh`. Old school sites are two thirds of the points and are hidden in the app unless the user ticks "Old school sites too".
- **Old mines and quarries** (`tools/mines.py`, grey dots). Past producers and prospects from the Mineral Resources Data System, which the USGS stopped updating in 2011. Working operations, bare mineral occurrences, wells and sand and gravel pits are left out. One download covers the country, and every state is in `STATES` in the tool.

What to remember when using either as a research lead: "historical" does not mean nothing is left (a historical school may be a house today); positions were read off old maps or mine reports and can be hundreds of metres out; and a mine record says nothing about who owns the land or whether the workings are safe. A lead becomes a place only by the rules of section 5.

### A province, territory or country that is not listed yet

Every US state and Canadian province was set up this way in November 2026, so this is only needed for somewhere new (a Canadian territory, Mexico, Puerto Rico). It needs five things besides its batch of places, all in the same pull request:

1. **Outline.** `python3 tools/outlines.py <key>` writes it into `build/states.json` (needs `pip install pyshp shapely`). It reads the Census 1:20,000,000 state file for the US and Natural Earth 1:10,000,000 for Canada, pushes the shape out about 3 km so that pins on a shore or a near island still fall inside, cuts away every neighbour's land, simplifies it, and stores each ring closed as a flat list `[lon, lat, lon, lat, …]`. For another country, add its source to `load()` in that file. State detection (which imagery and which law note apply) and `check.py` read these outlines; nothing draws them, so they are coarse. Outlines already in the file are left alone unless you name their key.
2. **Entry in `tools/states.py`:** key, name, `imagery` (say "fallback" unless you add state services), `owners`, `legal: False`, its region codes, and at least one start city (`hubs`) with coordinates. The build works out drive times from every start city.
3. **Map limits.** `MAXB` near the top of `source/src/app.js` is the box the map can be moved inside, as `[[south, west], [north, east]]`. It now runs from 17.5° N to 72° N and from 170° W to 50° W, which holds Hawaii, Alaska and Newfoundland. Widen it only for somewhere outside that. If a pin is outside the box, `check.py` fails and the map springs back before reaching it. `minZoom` is 3, which shows the whole box on a phone.
4. **`tools/pincheck.py`** takes each pin's aerial from `tools/ortho.py`, which picks by the state the pin is in (`SRC`, one row a state) and falls back to Esri satellite when the state has no row or its answer is blank. If you add state imagery, add its row there. `python3 tools/ortho.py --which <lat> <lon>` says what a point would get, and `tools/start.py` prints it for the place a session is starting on.
5. **Known gaps.** Write in section 10 what the state does not have yet (state imagery, owner lookup, lines, leads).

**The live site and the installed app do not show any of this until the pull request is merged.** The Android app carries its own copy of `app.js`, so a change to the map limits or settings reaches the phone only with the next app build (the pull request's `app` check publishes a test build; the merge publishes the release). New places alone arrive through the app's data refresh without a new build.

**Where the map opens.** Nobody has to pick a start city for a far-away area. The map reopens where it was last left, and until the reader chooses a start place by hand, drive times count from the start city nearest to their location (or last known location). **A reader with no start place at all** (a first visit, with location off) is not treated as living in Albany, which is only the first city in the list: `homePicked()` in `app.js` is false until a start place is chosen by hand (`U.home.by` 1) or set from the reader's position (2). Until then the map opens on the whole country with the pins grouped (`USBOX` in `startView()`); the list is in region order and shows no drive times; a place page shows no time and its directions link names no origin; there is no start pin; the trip builder asks for a start place before it plans; and a card at the head of the Places list (`startCard()`) asks "Where are you starting from?" with three answers: "Use my location" (the map then goes to town scale, zoom 10, not street scale), "Choose a place" (Settings, at the start place, which reads "Not chosen yet") and "Not now" (`U.ui.noStart`; the card does not come back, and nothing is measured until a start place is set under the gear). Anything new that shows a time or a distance from the start place must ask `fromOK()` first (a start place, or the reader's position while it is on). Test: the "first visit" part of `tools/places_test.js`. A reader whose device has no location (a desktop with location turned off) can choose a start city, pick a spot on the map, or type a town, address, ZIP code or coordinates under the gear in Map options. Typed names are looked up with OpenStreetMap's Nominatim; typed coordinates are not sent anywhere.

**Drive times.** `tools/drive.py` gives each place a drive time only from the start cities within 450 miles in a straight line. Beyond that, and where no road joins the two (Hawaii's other islands from Honolulu), the app shows its own rough estimate marked `~`. Adding a start city to `states.py` is enough; the next build works out its times. **How they reach the reader (November 22, 2026):** the hosted page no longer carries the times. They were a fifth of the page (every place's time from every start city within range) and a reader uses one city's worth, so `make.py` takes `dt` off each place and writes one file per start city, `data/dt/<city>-<stamp>.json` (`{place id: [minutes, miles]}`), with the stamps listed in the page as `dtx`. `dt(s)` in `app.js` asks for the chosen city's file the first time a time is wanted (`dtLoad()`, kept in `DTT`), shows its own estimate marked `~` until the file is in, then redraws; a reader with no start place fetches none. The page went from 3.3 MB to 2.7 MB over the wire; a city's file is about 20 KB as a rule and 125 KB at the largest, before compression. The service worker keeps the files with the old lines (`sw.js`), and the Android app fetches and keeps them like the per-state layers (`boot.js`). The two single-file copies keep the times inside, as before. An Android app older than this change has no `dtLoad()`: once it takes in the newer place data it shows estimated times (`~`) until the app itself is updated. After changing any of this run `tools/places_test.js` (the "drive times" checks) and `tools/app_smoke.js`.

**Canada.** The app, the pin check, the place lookup (`tools/nom.py`) and drive times work the same there. Three things do not exist for it: USGS old maps (`topo_all.py` prints `no map`, which is expected), the PAD-US public-land lookup (so a Canadian place never shows a land line; say who manages the land in the access note), and any law note.

**Old-map crops.** `tools/topo_all.py` cuts a crop for every new place except those in the `landmark` category, which it skips on purpose (a standing landmark is on the current map). If a `landmark` place is really a trace on the ground, such as trail ruts, and an old map would help, give it a more specific category or pass its id after removing the skip for that run; do not report the missing crop as a failure.


## 9. Changing the app

- `app.js` is one file with sections marked by comments. State is the object `U`; `save()` persists it. Add new saved fields to `U0()` so old backups still load.
- The phone layout is the main one: a scrolling icon bar under the map, an options sheet with a fixed Done button, five tabs (Places, Near, Trip, Log, Guide). Keep touch targets large. Test at phone size first.
- The app must still work when a data file fails to load or the phone is offline. Show a short message; never a blank screen.
- Bump nothing by hand: `make.py` stamps a version into `index.html`, the script URL and `sw.js` on every build.
- The app also builds as a single self-contained file (`dist/Lost-and-Hidden-Atlas.html`). It detects that mode by the presence of an inline `d-topo` block.
- **Settings** live under the gear in the map options sheet (`buildSettings()`). Each one is a key in `SET0`, read with `pref(key)` and stored in `U.set`; add a new setting by adding its default to `SET0`, a row in `buildSettings()`, and a `pref()` call where it applies. Distances go through `fmtMi()`/`miTxt()` and clock times pass `hour12:H12()`, so new text respects the units and clock settings. Backups, the app's data source and offline storage are the collapsible sections at the end of Settings (`dataSections()`), not in the Guide. Rows go in through `add(...)`, which drops the empty ones: `Element.append(null)` prints the word "null", and did so on the website until November 2026. `openSettings('Near me')` opens the sheet at a heading. **What belongs where:** a choice made once and left alone goes in Settings (Near me's distances and the episode kit's base map and closing lines are there, even though the Near tab and the kit also show them); a switch you flip while looking at the map goes in Map options (`buildSheet()`). Map options lists every switch and nothing else: the small print under each (`small` and `.snote`) and the legends of layers that are off show only after "What do these do?" (`U.ui.tips`). Keep it that way when adding a layer, and run `tools/settings_test.js` after changing either sheet.
- **Colour schemes** (Settings: "Colours", "Light colours", "Dark colours"; test: `tools/theme_test.js`). The reader picks one light scheme and one dark scheme, and whether the phone decides between them or one is always used (`theme`: `auto`, `light`, `dark`; `themeL`; `themeD`; `themeNow()` and `applyTheme()` in `app.js`, which also follows the phone into night mode while the page is open). Nine schemes since November 18, 2026. Light: Brick and paper (the default), Slate and copper, Survey green, Daylight (high contrast). Dark: Tomorrow (the default, the Tomorrow Night palette), Tomorrow on true black (for OLED screens, where black pixels are off), Tomorrow morning, Blueprint night, Black and yellow (high contrast). The magenta scheme the Atlas began with is gone. **To add a scheme:** one block of the same seventeen values at the top of `app.css` under `:root[data-theme="<key>"]`, and one line in `THEMES` in `app.js` giving its key, its name and `l` or `d`; both Settings lists are made from `THEMES`, so nothing else changes. A high-contrast scheme also sets `--btnline`, `--btnw` and `--chipline` for heavier outlines. Then run `theme_test.js`, which chooses every scheme and holds it to WCAG contrast (7 to 1 for body text; 4.5 to 1 for quiet text, accent text, chips and text on an accent button). A setting saved by the build of the evening of November 17, which kept one name for mode and scheme, is split once by `themeMigrate()`. The same pass made every button bolder and fills the chosen map layer with the accent. Not checked: the map's own colours (pins, trolley pink, status colours) against each scheme beyond a look at five of them; a real phone; whether true black saves battery that anyone would notice.
- **The track colour** (Settings, "Track colour"; `trk`; test: `tools/settings_test.js`). One colour for everything the reader draws or follows: the field-log track and its note pins, the recording bar, and the Near tab's "point me there" arrow, ring and dashed line, which follow a change at once. Blue (`#2563eb`) by default; Red, Purple, Teal and Black to choose from. The old pink (`#e11d74`) was taken out on November 18, 2026 at the owner's request; a saved pink comes back as blue (`themeMigrate()`). The trolley lines' magenta (`LK.tr`) is a different colour and was left. On the panels (the Near arrow, the recording bar and its dot, the chosen note) the colour is `--trk-ui`: the track colour unless it would not show against the panel, as Black does in a dark scheme, where the text colour stands in (`trkUI()`, contrast under 2). The ring and the dashed line are each drawn over a white casing, as the track is, so they read on any imagery (`NM.ringBg`, `NM.lineBg`).
- **Pins** (November 18, 2026; test: `tools/places_test.js`). A pin's colour is the kind of place (`CAT` in `app.js`) and its shape is what is left: a star for standing (an inline SVG, `STARD`, drawn in `pinIcon()`, with a dark outline under a white one so it reads on any aerial), a solid circle for ruins and traces, a ring for nothing visible, a marker only or under water. List dots and the badge on a place page use the same shapes (`dot()`), and a short key sits at the top of Map options. Landmarks (`landmark`) are gold (`#f2b705`) and civic buildings (`civic`) periwinkle (`#5c6bc0`); until then they were grey and dark gold, and the grey squares were the least visible pins on the map. Places of worship (`worship`, added November 19, 2026) are wine red (`#9b1b45`). The names of the kinds changed that day too; section 4 has the table. The precision badge reads "Pin on the spot", "Pin within the site" or "Pin approximate". On a phone the layer bar ends in an arrow (`.lmore`) where more layers are off the edge, instead of a cut-off button. Not checked on a real phone. A place of the reader's own is a diamond whatever is left of it, on the map and in the lists (`.pin.mine`, `.dot.mine`): the star is for the Atlas's standing places only. A standing place that is changing now pulses like any other pin (a third path, `.p`, `starpulse`). The layer bar's arrow is worked out again when the window changes size (`buildBar.fit`).
- **Photos on place pages** (November 18, 2026; test: `tools/photo_test.js`; setting `wikiPhotos`, on by default, under the gear). When a place page opens and the place has a link to an English Wikipedia article (not a list), the app asks Wikipedia for the article's own chosen picture (`pageimages`, free licences only) and its coordinates, then asks Wikipedia for that file's photographer and licence. It shows the picture only when all of this holds (`photoFits()` and `placePhoto()` in `app.js`): the article's coordinates are within 1 km of the pin; the article is about this place, meaning its whole title is in the place's name, or the title shares a word with the name that is not a word of the town or a common building word (this keeps out the article on the town, whose picture is a skyline); the file is not an .svg or a map, seal, flag or plan; and a photographer and licence came back. The caption names the article, the photographer and licence, and links the file's page. The photo sits under the badges and scrolls with the page. Nothing is asked when offline, for the reader's own places, or with the setting off; an answer is kept for the visit and a failure is asked again next time. A sample of 48 places on November 18 gave 36 photos; the 12 left out had no picture, no coordinates or an article on something larger. Nothing is stored in the repository. The picture carries its width and height, so its box is the right height before it arrives. A long plain credit (an archive's full name) is cut to about 68 characters; a credit that is a banner with no name in it gives no picture.
- **Photo details ahead of time** (November 19, 2026; `tools/photos.py`, `src/photos.json`, test: `tools/photo_test.js`). Asking Wikipedia when a place page opens made the page jump when the picture arrived, and cost two requests a page. `tools/photos.py` now asks ahead, fifty places to a request, by the same rules as `placePhoto()` (keep the two in step: `title_of`/`wikiTitle`, `fits`/`photoFits`, `by`/`photoBy`), and writes `src/photos.json`: for each place the picture's address, size, photographer, licence, the file's page and the article title, or 0 for "looked, nothing usable". `make.py` ships it as `data/photos.json`; the app fetches it once, a moment after the page opens (`phxLoad()`), and `photoBox()` then puts a known picture in the page from the first frame, with its width and height, and asks Wikipedia nothing. A place the file does not hold (written since the last run) is asked for as before. The tool needs the network and Wikipedia makes cloud machines wait (HTTP 429 with a Retry-After, which the tool obeys), so it is not part of the build: run `python3 tools/photos.py` every week or two from a maintainer session (it only looks up places not yet in the file; `--all` starts over) and commit the file. Only addresses and credits are stored, never a picture.
- **A tap on the photo shows it large** (`photoBig()`): the same picture over the whole page with its credit, a bigger copy asked for where the address is a Wikimedia thumbnail; a tap or Escape puts it away. Under it every place page has **More photos** (`morePhotos()`), which opens an image search for the place's name, town and state in the reader's browser. Nothing from that search comes into the Atlas, so there is no licence to worry about. Test: `tools/photo_test.js`. **It can be pinched bigger (November 25, 2026):** two fingers make it up to six times its size, one finger slides it, a double tap makes it bigger or takes it back, a mouse wheel does the same (`PZ`, `pzSet`, `pzAt`). The page itself never zooms, so this is done by hand on the picture. A sharper copy (1920 wide) is asked for only once it is pinched past one and a half times. A single tap puts an unzoomed picture away; a zoomed one is put away by the ✕ in the corner, Escape or the phone's Back button (`atlasBack`). The credit stays along the bottom. `tools/photo_test.js` checks all of it with real two-finger touches. **In the Android app the photo details come from the Atlas address (November 25, 2026):** `data/photos.json` is not built into the app, and `A.fetch` in `boot.js` used to look for it only inside the app, so the app had none: a picture found on Wikimedia Commons never showed there, and every other place asked Wikipedia each time. It is now fetched like the stone walls (the Atlas address first, the kept copy with no signal); `tools/app_smoke.js` checks it.
- **The finder can log in to Commons** (November 22, 2026; `login()`, `_sess()`, `pace()` in `tools/photo_find.py`; test: `tools/photo_find_test.py`). Since 2026 Wikimedia limits a tool known only by its address to a few hundred API questions an hour and allows a logged-in one several thousand (mediawiki.org, "Wikimedia APIs/Rate limits"); the pictures job was held to about ten a minute. The owner made an ordinary Commons account and, at `Special:BotPasswords`, a bot password with basic rights only (it cannot edit or upload, and he can delete it there at any time). The login is read from two environment variables, `ATLAS_COMMONS_USER` (the name Commons gives, `Account@botname`) and `ATLAS_COMMONS_PASS`, which he sets on the cloud environment the pictures job runs in. **Neither value ever goes into the repository, a note, a pull request, a task's instructions or anything printed:** the tool says only that it logged in or that it could not. With the variables set, the first question logs in, each later one carries `assert=user` (so a lapsed login is noticed and renewed once, not silently answered at the anonymous allowance), and questions are spaced 2 seconds apart (`PACE_IN`) in place of 6.5. Without them, or if the login is refused, everything is as before. A refusal with a wait is still obeyed either way. Every run of the count, `next`, `look` and `new` ends with one line, `login_note()`, saying which it was: logged in, a login set but refused, or none set in the environment; it is the last line so that a log cut short still shows it (the first run after the owner set the variables left him unable to tell). The login was seen working on November 22, 2026 (Commons mailed the owner about it, and the run printed the line). **The questions were never the slow part, and neither was any limit on preview pictures.** A round of five places still took ten minutes with the login on, so the tool now also ends with `time_note()`: how many questions and how long, how many preview pictures (the small copies for the sheets, from upload.wikimedia.org) and how long, and how many were refused. That line showed three refusals of a minute each at nearly every place, logged in or not, and the maintainer first took it for a limit of about five previews a minute and was about to halve the candidates on a sheet. Fetching the previews one at a time and printing each answer showed otherwise: fifteen of sixteen came at once, and one was refused every time, with "Too many requests ... instead use thumbnails" and a wait of ten minutes. For a picture narrower than the 900 pixels the tool asks for, Commons gives the address of the file itself, `thumb330()` left such an address alone, and Commons refuses a tool that fetches files themselves. One such candidate cost a place three minutes and left a grey tile on its sheet (the "thumbnail did not render" in several notes). `thumb330()` now makes a small-copy address out of a file's own address: the same two places went from 182 and 187 seconds of previews to 2 and 1. The lesson: a time line says where the time went, not why; look at one request before believing a rate. **A second look for places that may have lost out** (`SEARCH` is now 3; `asked_again()`, `seen_whole()`): 267 places had been given "none" by a session after a look under the second search, and some of those sessions never saw the one candidate that fitted, because it was the grey box. Such a place is offered once more, but a session is only asked to look where it can matter: the tool gathers the candidates again, and if every one is a small copy (so all were on the earlier sheet) it carries the none forward by itself under the new number. Only a place with a candidate whose address is the file itself gets a sheet and a look. A place Commons had nothing for is not asked again.
- **The finder was widened, and the rule for choosing turned round** (November 21 and 22, 2026; `gather()`, `named()`, `queries()`, `pick()`, `SEARCH` in `tools/photo_find.py`). The owner saw too few pictures arriving: of 1,043 places looked at, 412 had got one, and 338 were recorded "nothing on Commons". Ten of those were checked by hand and five had photographs the tool never offered. What was wrong, and what it does now: (1) it refused TIFF files, and the Library of Congress building surveys (HABS, HAER, HALS; public domain, tens of thousands of mills, bridges and houses) are TIFF on Commons, each with a JPEG copy the Atlas can show: now offered, drawing sheets left out, and a survey credit "Lowe, Jet, creator" written "Jet Lowe"; (2) it asked Commons only for a place's whole title with its town, so "Adirondac (Tahawus) and McIntyre furnace, Newcomb" found nothing though thirty survey photographs are filed under "McIntyre Iron Works ... Tahawus": it now also asks for each part of a long title by itself, counts the hamlet in brackets as the town, and leaves "site", "ruins" and "the" out of the question, three questions a place at most; (3) every such question came back full of scanned books and newspapers, which Commons searches by every word in them: the question now leaves PDF and DjVu files out; (4) Commons gives the position of ten files a question unless asked for more (`colimit`), so the tool saw at most ten photographs near any pin and took the rest for files with no position: it asks for all, and reads an answer that comes in two parts; (5) it opened no category: the photographs in a category whose title names the place are now offered; (6) a photograph taken beyond the pin's reach was never offered: one taken within 600 m (1,000 m for a site or an area) is, when its own words name the place, because a lighthouse or a fort is photographed from afar; (7) a name had to match in every word and with the town: a name of three words or more that is one word short, or a name of two or more with no town, is offered marked LOOK TWICE, as is one whose file names another state (the Florida church for the Georgia one), and "MesierHomestead.JPG" is read as two words. Each candidate says how it was found, the choice records it as an eighth item in its row (`pin`, `pin+name`, `name`, `category`, `far`, `loose`), and `photo_find.py sheet --unsure` makes sheets of every picture chosen on `far` or `loose` for a person to look over. **Choosing:** the owner, November 21: "it's better to have a false positive than it is to not have a picture of a building that has one", since a wrong picture is easy for a person to spot. So a session chooses when a candidate plausibly shows the place and says none only when none plausibly does; the nevers (an interior, a plaque, a person, a plainly different building) stand. **Empty places cost nobody a look:** `photo_find.py next 5` records "none" by itself for a place with no candidate at all and shows sheets only for places that have some. **Asked again:** a "none" row now ends with the number of the search it was recorded under (`SEARCH`, now 2); every "none" from before is offered once more. Raise `SEARCH` when the finder is widened again. **Commons' allowance:** it answers a client a few dozen questions a minute and then HTTP 429 with a wait; every Atlas session running in the same hour shares it (18 questions in a row got 8 answers). The tool keeps 6.5 seconds between questions (under ten a minute), waits as told on a 429, and stops asking about a place once three photographs name it. The first unattended batch with the wider finder, at 2.5 seconds, reached only 20 places in 40 minutes and chose 5 pictures: timed afterwards, 234 of 274 seconds for eight places were spent waiting out refusals (every fourth question refused, 45 seconds each), and at 4 seconds 6 of 24 were still refused. Wikimedia's own page gives 10 questions a minute to a client it knows only by its address, 200 to one with a compliant name and more to a logged-in bot; this tool is plainly held to the first. A logged-in bot account would lift it and is the owner's to decide (asked November 22, 2026). Do not run two finders at once from one session, and do not give the tool a different name to Commons to get a second allowance: that is going round their limit. **Not done:** nothing outside Commons is looked at (street-level photographs, the owner's or readers' own); a search cannot find a file whose name is run together unless its description spells the name; a place Commons files under a name the Atlas does not give (a house known by a later owner) is still missed; and the owner's hope of nine places in ten will not be met from Commons alone, because cellar holes, LiDAR finds and small rural ruins have no photograph there.
- **Pictures found for places that have none** (November 20, 2026; `tools/photo_find.py`, `tools/pics_<four letters>.py`, tests `tools/photo_find_test.py` and `tools/photo_test.js`). The article pictures above reach only places whose own Wikipedia article has a usable lead image, about half. For the rest a session can look on Wikimedia Commons. **The tool decides everything that can be decided without looking:** only Commons; only a free licence and a photographer to credit; never a map, a plan, an emblem or a sliver of a panorama; only a photograph taken within reach of the pin (150 m exact, 250 m site, 400 m area) and, always, ones whose own name and description hold the town and every telling word of the place's name (shown as "by name" and listed first; one that carries a position more than 1.5 km off is a namesake and is dropped; when a name's only telling word is its town, as in "Charlottetown City Hall", the plain words must be there too). The address, the size, the credit and the licence are copied from Commons by the tool. **The session decides one thing, by looking at the sheet:** which candidate, if any, shows this place from outside. "None" is recorded too, with what the candidates showed, so the place is not offered again. The first six places tried show why the look matters: beside one church the nearest photograph was the city hall, one candidate near a stove works was a man playing a guitar, and nine photographs filed at one museum's position were of a village ten miles away. `tools/make.py` folds the choices into `data/photos.json` (`photo_find.merge()`): a picture from the place's own article wins, and a found one has no article title, which is how the page knows to credit it "Photo: who · licence · Wikimedia Commons" (`photoBox()`). `check.py` refuses a pics file whose picture is not at Wikimedia, whose licence is not free, or whose credit holds an address. A pull request holding only pics files merges by itself; the sample check then makes one sheet of the whole batch (`photo_find.py sheet`) and looks at every tile. **A research session does this for its own new places** (the same day; the owner: "I don't need to go back into a second batch"): `photo_find.py new <its data file>` asks Wikipedia about the batch's linked places only (`photos.lookup()`, the asking half of `tools/photos.py`, which writes nothing), lists the places with no article and those whose article has no picture the Atlas may show, and the session looks and chooses as above; its pics file rides in the same pull request. A place whose article has a picture needs nothing: the page fetches it until the next run of `tools/photos.py` stores it. Tried on the capitols batch: 65 of 70 had an article picture and 5 needed a look. **Trying a cheaper model on pins** (the same day): `pincheck.py id@lat,lon` draws a place's tile at a point of your choosing, with nothing in the title to say how sure the pin is, so a model can be shown pins whose right answer is already known. **Comparing two models** (the same day): `photo_find.py try <id> <n>` or `try <id> none "<why>"` is `set` without the recording. It writes one line to `research/trial.md` and nothing that reaches the map, so a cheaper model can be run over places another has already judged and the two answers laid side by side (`trial_read()`). **The first unattended batch** (Sonnet, 30 places, the same day): 8 pictures, every one right when looked at afterwards; 18 "none", of which 8 had nothing on Commons at all and the rest were sound refusals bar two borderline ones; and 4 landmark pins lost because `check.py` refused `lm-` ids. So the count was held down by the search, not by the judging, and three things changed: the by-name search runs for every place (it used to run only when fewer than four photographs were taken near the pin, which hid a city hall's eleven daytime photographs behind two night shots); a "none" recorded before November 20, 2026 is offered once more (`asked_again()`; the twenty recorded that morning under the narrow search were taken out of their files by hand, so that the later answers of the same day stand), and where two files answer for one place the later day wins; a landmark pin may have a picture (`id_ok()`). An error inside a reply from Commons is retried and then stops the command; it is never read as "nothing here" (`refused()`), because that would be written down as a permanent "none".
- **The heat map is what a first visit shows, and the step to pins no longer holds the page** (the same day; the owner: "switch the heat map to the main setting. You can leave the bubbles as a backup", "start the lag pass", "make it switch to the pins slightly earlier"). `SET0.heat` is true; the numbered discs are the backup, under the gear ("Zoomed out, show a heat map", off for discs). With heat the pins came at zoom 9 (`HEATZ`=8) for a few hours, a step sooner than after the discs; the owner had that put back the same day (`HEATZ`=9, pins from zoom 10, as with the discs), because zoom 9 round Albany put 1,350 pins on the map and a pinch there crawled. A favourite over the heat is full size, there being no number to hide. **The lag** he felt at the switch was measured: that step put every pin within a screen's width of the view on the map in one go, about 800 round Albany, and took 1,061 ms on a browser slowed four times, nearly all of it Leaflet placing the icons and the browser laying them out. `syncMarkers()` now puts on the 80 new pins nearest the middle of the view at once (`PINFIRST`) and the rest outwards 30 a frame (`PINLOT`); a pin already on the map is only brought up to date; a pin no longer wanted comes off at once if it is in or near the view and a lot a frame if it is beyond the edges; a later call drops what is still waiting (`pinGen`). Round Albany the switch now gives the page back in about 200 ms on the same slowed browser, with 1,300 pins at the earlier zoom; Boston about 115 ms, Kansas City about 85 ms. Checks in `tools/places_test.js` (a first lot, nearest the middle, then all of them; no pin left behind when the map moves before the ring is filled). The tests that are about the discs start with `set:{heat:false}` in the stored settings. **Not done in this pass:** the first open (the page is about 10 MB, 3.2 MB over the wire), scrolling the Places list, opening a place page; a zoom step with more than a thousand pins already on the map still takes about 350 ms slowed, most of it Leaflet moving every icon.
- **Old town centres are drawn over the heat map, and named from zoom 8** (November 22, 2026; `drawHeat()`, `pockLabels()`; test: `tools/overlay_test.js`, the pockets block). The heat map's pane was above the pane the old lines, the lead dots and the town discs are drawn in (390 over 380), so at zoom 8 and 9 the discs were tinted by it and "looked a little weird" (the owner). The heat pane is now 370: the heat is the ground colour and whatever the reader has switched on lies over it. The names by the best town centres used to start at zoom 10; they now start where the discs do, zoom 8 (`POCKZ`), up to 18 of them below zoom 10, best score first and none overlapping, because zoomed out the street map's own town names are too small to read and a name is how the right town is found. The discs still start at zoom 8, as he settled on November 19.
- **Pinch lag: one fix tried and taken out the same day** (November 22, 2026). The owner: round Albany the map "runs really well when you're not pinching, but the second you touch it with 2 fingers, it starts running very slowly". What is known: a one-finger drag slides the whole map as one sheet, but at every frame of a pinch Leaflet's markers answer the `zoom` event by working out their place and writing it and their stacking order again, every pin on the map, in view or not (790 pins at zoom 10 round Albany, 1,330 at zoom 9). The fix tried (#481) replaced `L.Marker.prototype.update` so that on a pinch frame only the pins in or near the view were moved and the rest were left where they were until the fingers lifted. In the test browser (headless, slowed four times) it halved the frame time, 85 to 48 ms at zoom 10. **On the owner's phone it felt much worse than before, and it was reverted (#481's revert).** The lesson is about the measuring, not only the fix: the test browser draws in software and a phone draws with its graphics chip, so a frame time from the test browser does not say how a pinch will feel on a phone. Nothing about pinching should be merged again on the strength of the test browser alone; the owner has to feel it on his phone first, or the app has to time real frames on the phone. Also tried in the test browser and no better there: hiding the off-screen pins for the length of the pinch; `will-change` on the pins, on the pane or on the tiles; moving pins with a flat `translate()` or with `left`/`top`; stretching the whole marker pane as one sheet. One thing reached the no-pins frame time there: taking the marker pane off the page while two fingers are down and showing one drawn picture of the pins in its place (the pins then stretch with the map until the fingers lift). Untested on a phone. To reproduce a pinch in a test, do what Leaflet's handler does: `map._moveStart(true,false)`, then `map._move(centre, zoom, {pinch:true, round:false})` once a frame, then `map._animateZoom(centre, whole zoom, true, true)`; two touch points sent with `Input.dispatchTouchEvent` also work when both are in the first `touchStart`.
- **While two fingers are down, one picture stands in for the pins** (November 22, 2026; the setting `pinchStand`, "Smooth pinching" under the gear, on unless switched off; `pinchPaint()`, `pinchFrame()`, `pinchAnim()`, `pinchEnd()`, `pinDraw()` in `app.js`; test: `tools/overlay_test.js`, block 7). It began the same day as a trial, off by default, because the owner asked to see the idea before it was built in full; he tried it on his phone ("that completely 100% fixed how it feels") and it became the default. Why it works where moving fewer pins did not: a pinch is smooth on his phone while the heat map shows, and the heat map is one picture stretched. At the first frame of a pinch (a `zoom` event that says `pinch`) `pinchPaint()` draws the place pins in the view and a third of a screen beyond onto one canvas, puts it in a pane of its own over the marker pane, and hides the place pins with one class on the marker pane (`.pinch-on .pinwrap{display:none}`); other markers, such as the "you are here" dot and the town names, stay as they are. At each later frame only the canvas's transform is set, the way Leaflet stretches its tiles. Leaflet goes on moving the hidden pins, so when the fingers lift (`zoomanim` glides the canvas to the whole zoom step, then `zoomend` or `moveend`) the canvas is taken away and the pins are shown where they belong. The price: the pins grow and shrink with the map during the pinch. **Drawn again when stretched far:** a picture stretched a long way goes soft (he saw it on the stars after a long pinch) and one shrunk a long way no longer reaches the edges, so once the stretch passes 1.4 or falls under 0.7 the picture is painted afresh for the map as it then stands, with the pins drawn at the size they had grown or shrunk to (`mag`), so nothing jumps. **`pinDraw()` draws a pin as `app.css` does** (round, hollow, star, the reader's own diamond, favourite, chosen, the green and orange status rings, the faded ones, the small size below zoom 12); a pulsing pin gets a steady ring, as for a reader who asked for less motion. A change to how a pin looks in `app.css` needs the same change in `pinDraw()`, and the way to check is to look: the real pins and the picture side by side, enlarged. Doing that found one fault on the page itself: a star with a status ring had a square drawn round it (the round pins' ring, applied to the star's square box); a star now shows its status in its outline only. The test checks that the swap is right, not that it is fast: only a phone says that. **Not done, on purpose:** the double-tap zoom, the step from heat map to pins and a change of filters still handle each pin by itself; the same picture could stand in during the double-tap glide if that starts to drag, and drawing every pin as one picture all the time is a rewrite (taps, name labels, dragging a pin and most tests assume a pin is an element) that the owner chose to leave until it is needed.
- **The heat map, a trial** (November 22, 2026; `heatPlan()`, `drawHeat()`, `heatTap()`, the `heat` setting; checks in `tools/places_test.js`). The owner felt lag where the numbered discs give way to pins, did not want the discs kept to closer zooms ("it makes it a little harder to find the clusters of pins"), and asked for "a smooth thing by colour instead of bubbles". Under the gear, "Zoomed out, show a heat map instead of numbered discs" (off unless switched on; it needs "Group pins" on). Up to zoom 9 the places are counted in squares of 16 screen pixels and each square is painted as a soft blob; the blobs run together and their strength becomes colour, blue for a few through teal, yellow and orange to red. "Full" is the count nine squares in ten fall under, not the fullest square: judged by Albany's square the rest of the continent was a grey wash. The discs' own purple and rose went muddy over the base map, so the ramp is the usual one. It is one picture (an image overlay a screen and a half wide, drawn at half size), made again when the zoom, the filters or the ground change: about 10 ms. A favourite and the chosen place keep their pins; a tap on colour says about how many places are round the finger and that a double-tap goes closer. **Measured** on a browser slowed four times, zoom steps by level, discs then heat: zoom 8, 272 and 174 ms; zoom 9, 355 and 173 ms. **It does not cure the lag at the switch:** the step to zoom 10 took 514 ms with discs and 668 ms with heat, because that step puts some 250 pins on the map at once (every place within a screen of the view) and with heat none of them is there beforehand. That is the first thing for the speed pass. **Not done:** no count is shown until tapped; the picture is not redrawn while a pinch is in progress, only when it ends; the colours are the same in every colour scheme; nobody has tried it on a phone.
- **The audit before the owner's reveal video** (the maintainer, November 22, 2026; he asked for a look for bugs, "I need it to be good"). Every test was run, every screen photographed at phone size and looked at, and the text of every place read by pattern. What was found and changed: (1) **Workshop talk on place pages.** About 90 access notes said "would not open in this session", 170 pin notes named a tool ("OpenStreetMap bridge (tools/nom.py)", "moved with pix.py") and 130 said "prec is site". `tools/plainwords.py` now rewrites such wording when the place data is built (`build_sites.py` passes every place's story, look, access, hours and pin note through it; test `tools/plainwords_test.py`), so the sources stay as written and text written later is put right too; wording only, never a fact. After changing it run the test, and after a build no reader field should match `this session`, `\.py\b` or `\bprec\b`. (2) **Notes addressed to the owner** in the first Albany entries ("Included for completeness; you have likely filmed it", "thriving in your era", "film from Burden Ave", "Confirm dates before filming", "Pair with Schuyler Flatts burials", "out on the southern edge of your range") were reworded in `src/catalogue.md`, `src/entries.json` (the catalogue is not parsed again at build: change both), `src/landmarks.json` and `data_a/b/c.py`. (3) **Bubbles on top of each other.** A numbered disc is drawn at the middle of its places, not of its grid square, so neighbours overlapped: on the opening map New York's 1,830 hid New England's 917. `clusterPlan()` now joins discs that would overlap, the larger taking the smaller, until none do; and because a joined disc can cover ground as wide as the view, a tap on a disc zooms one step when fitting its ground would not move the map (`tools/places_test.js` checks five views for overlap, that every place is still counted, and that a tap goes closer). (4) **Two places were on the map twice:** Quindaro (the kc2 entry now has `was='quindaro-townsite'`) and Vale Cemetery (`was='lm-vale-cemetery'`). Three pairs still share one point and hide each other, each a different subject on the same ground: Delavan House site and Kiernan Plaza; the Hampden Railroad and the Southern New England Railway at Three Rivers; Queen's Fort and the Stockade district. Not changed. (5) **Small wording on screen:** a fix's accuracy is given in the reader's units (`accFmt()`; it said "good to 10 m" beside distances in feet); the Colours menu's first choice is "Follow the phone" and the Near tab's alert menu says "Alert at 330 ft", because the longer words were cut off in the closed menu; the tags on a place page are centred in their row (`.dmeta`), where "pin within the site" had been stretched with its words at the top. (6) `tools/shots.js` looked for the Trip tab's "Use my location" button, gone since the trip began from where you are; it now sets the leaving time to 9:30 so the picture shows a morning out. The twelve pictures were retaken and the front page's "More than 3,500 places" made 4,500. (7) A stray `smoke-joliet-iron-works.png` in the repository root was removed. **Seen and left:** a place page's photograph is fetched from Wikimedia, and when Wikimedia refuses (it throttles an address that asks a lot, as this workspace did during the audit) the page shows no picture and says nothing; a phone is not throttled that way. 383 places marked open have no hours row yet (the hours task is working through them). **Not verified:** a real phone, and the Android app, for any of it.
- **The feature page** (November 20, 2026; the owner wanted "a thing I can point to" on a live stream, with pictures, and on the GitHub page). `README.md` opens with twelve phone screenshots, one line each, then how to use the Atlas and what each layer covers. `tools/shots.js` takes the pictures from a served build, each from a new, empty browser, with "you are here" set to a made-up spot on a public street; they live in `docs/features/`. After a change that alters how one of those screens looks, run it, look at every picture, copy them across, and check each line of the page still says what the screen shows. A photograph that appears in a screenshot is credited under the table.
- **Placing by hand, and spots** (November 20, 2026, after the owner's first real field day; `placeAt`, `addBar`, `spotDrop`, `spotCard`, test `tools/spots_test.js`). "+ Add" used to say only "tap the map", with nothing to show where the pin would land. Now a cross stands at the middle of the map: move the map under it and press "Place here", or click or tap the spot. The form then opens with a pink pin that can still be dragged until the place is saved (`formPin`). **Spots** are the quick way to hand over a street of buildings: "Spot many" on the same bar, then every press of Drop leaves a bare numbered mark at the cross, with no form; "Where I am" drops one at the phone's position, Undo takes the last back. On a phone a tap on the map drops nothing in spot mode, because a slip of the thumb is not a spot; with a mouse a click does. Spots are `U.spots` (`[{lat,lon,t,note?}]`), drawn on the map and counted on a card in the Places tab until they are copied out ("Copy for Claude"; they also travel in "Copy my additions for Claude") and cleared. A spot can be given a few words by tapping it, and does not need them: the owner found a session can work out the building from the point alone. `tools/visits.py` prints each spot as a `LEADS.md` row, and `tools/start.py` lists the owner's spots first among the leads for a piece of ground, because he can tell at a glance what is old.
- **The head of a place page** (November 18, 2026; test: `tools/land_test.js`). Only the head stays put while a place page scrolls (`.dhead`): one row of buttons (← Back, + Add to trip, Zoom in) and the name. The line under the name, the badges and the photo are in `.dintro` and scroll away. On a phone on its side the name is smaller and cut to two lines, so the head is about 65 points of a 320-point column. Keep anything new out of `.dhead`: the owner found that in landscape a taller head left almost nothing to scroll. A name too long for the two lines is written in full at the top of the part that scrolls (`nameFit()`, `.fullname`), and that is worked out again when the window changes shape.
- **A double tap** (November 19, 2026; test: `tools/settings_test.js`; setting `dbl`, "A double tap on the map zooms in", one to four steps, three unless changed). Leaflet's own double tap goes in one step, which is slow work with one thumb, most of all with the map full screen, so it is switched off (`map.doubleClickZoom.disable()` in `initMap()`) and the Atlas handles `dblclick` itself. On open map: that many steps in, about the point tapped (Shift and a double click goes out). On a pin: its page opens (the first tap) and the map goes to street level, zoom 17, on the pin (`dblPin()`). On a numbered disc: a single tap still opens the group about two steps; a double tap goes the full number of steps toward the disc, counted from where the map stood before the first tap, or until the pins fill the view when the group is tight (`dblGroup()`, `dblFrom`). The first tap's zoom is still running when the second lands, so `dblGroup()` waits for `zoomend`; a view set earlier is undone when that zoom finishes.
- **Compare: Swipe and Overlay** (November 19, 2026; test: `tools/settings_test.js`). The owner had to open Map options to get from the divider to one layer lying over the other, and it was forgotten each time. The Compare button on the bar now steps round three stops, `cmpTap()`: off, the way used last, the other way, off; its label says which is showing (Compare, Swipe, Overlay). In Overlay a small slider on the map (`#fade`) sets how strongly the layer on top shows.
