Everything chosen is kept in `U.ui.cmp` (`cmpSave()`): on or off, the way, the second layer, the strength; a comparison left on is on again when the page opens. A tap that passes through the second way on the route to off (within a second and a half) does not make it the way to remember. The code calls Overlay `fade`. No message is shown over Overlay, because the slider sits where a message would.
- **Favourites** (November 19, 2026; test: `tools/places_test.js`). The reader's own short list, apart from the trip: the heart in a place page's top row (`favToggle()`, `U.fav`, ids in the order added, kept in the browser and in a backup). A favourite's pin wears a thick white ring and is a little bigger (`.pin.fav`), `clusterPlan()` never folds it into a grey group disc, so it stays on the map at every zoom, and it is never hidden as "nothing to see". Filters has "My favourites", among the shortcuts and under Only show (`F.fav`), and a list row says "favourite". When a full entry replaces a thin pin, `aliasMigrate()` carries the favourite across.
- **Adding a place: the quick card** (November 21, 2026; `quickAdd()`, `QWHAT`, `QKIND`, the setting `addMore`; test: `tools/spots_test.js`). "Place here", "Where I am" or a tap on the map opens one row of four taps, Standing, Ruin, Trace and Odd find, and a tap saves the place (named "Unnamed ruin (Nov 21)" and so on, `prec='exact'`, `acc='view'`; Odd find is the kind `curio`). Nothing is typed. The map is moved so the pink pin is in the middle of what still shows of it. With the setting "Adding a place also asks its kind, year and name" the card has kinds to tap, a year on a number pad and a name above the row. "More details" opens the full form, which is also what Edit and "+ Add to my places" on a lead or a field-log note open. The owner is trying both settings; the default may change.
- **A long press on the map, sending a place of your own, and no two pins for one place** (November 21, 2026; `onContext()`, `sendRow()`, `twins()`, `twinNote()`, `U.sent`, `U.keep`; test: `tools/spots_test.js`). The long-press box was taller than the map on a phone, so its lower buttons could not be reached: it now shows the coordinates, "+ Add to my places", "Send this spot to the public Atlas" and "Whose land is it?", with the links to other maps folded under "See this spot in other maps", and it never grows taller than the map. A place of the reader's own has "Send to the public Atlas" at the top of its page (it was far down, under My notes, as "Suggest it"); pressing a Send link is remembered. Once the public Atlas has that place, the reader's own copy steps aside, kept but not drawn or listed: when a public place marked "sent in by a reader" is within 80 m, or any public place is within 40 m of a copy that was sent. The public place's page says so, with "Show my copy" and "Delete my copy".
- **The filters, tidied (November 24, 2026; test in `tools/places_test.js`).** (1) **A year range** replaces "Dates from: before ...": two boxes, from and to (`F.y0`, `F.y1`; either may be empty; the same year twice is that year), with quick picks that only fill the boxes. It goes by `yearOf()`, the first year in a place's dates, and a place with no year is left out while it is on. Typing does not rebuild the panel under the fingers (`yearApply()`). (2) **Few places, every pin**: when a search or a filter leaves `FEWPINS` (400) places or fewer, `clusterPlan()` stands aside and each is its own pin however far out the map is; with nothing narrowed the discs or the heat map stand in as before. The number is a first guess for a phone: the owner tunes it by feel. (3) **Five shortcuts**, down from ten: newest, favourites, red brick, changing now, pins to confirm. Gone: ruins you can walk to and within 30 minutes (the Near tab), standing from before 1800 (the year range), and two that repeated a chip under Kind of place. The drive-time list itself now asks for a start place when there is none, as the shortcut did. (4) **Clear without scrolling**: "Clear all filters" at the top of the panel when anything is on, and "Clear filters" beside the count of places when the panel is shut. (5) **The bar of years** (`yearsBar()`, `yearSpan()`; the setting `U.ui.yrs`): shut until asked for, behind the word "Years" on the count line, because the owner's worry was clutter. Open, it is one bar a span of years for whatever is listed: 50 years across centuries (his choice to start), 25 across a couple, a decade across about one, single years inside a decade or so. A tap on a bar sets the year range to it and the bars then show that span more finely, which is how to see what one town built in one year; "All years" takes the range off. Places with no year are counted in the caption, not drawn. (6) **A date that begins "before" or "by" is not a year**: `yearOf()` returns it as a negative number, the latest year it can be ("before 1900" is -1899). Such a place answers "up to a year" and no range with a first year, and is in no bar. Before that a LiDAR find dated "before 1900–after 1946" was listed under 1900 to 1915; 95 places have such dates. He tried 327 pins at once on his phone (hotels and resorts, zoomed right out) and it was smooth, so `FEWPINS` stays at 400.
- **"Report a problem with this place"** (November 24, 2026; `problemSection()`, `PROBLEMS`, kind `problem` in `shareLinks()`; `.github/ISSUE_TEMPLATE/problem.yml`; test in `tools/visit_test.js`). At the foot of every place page that is not the reader's own, folded away: five reasons (not what the Atlas is for; gone or changed; pin in the wrong spot; a fact is wrong; something else) and one line more. It is not a visit report: nobody has to have been there. It travels the way the other reports do, by the owner's form (kind "A problem with a place", which the owner adds to the form's first question; until then the form simply leaves that question blank and `inbox.py` knows the report by its text beginning "Problem:") or by a GitHub issue labelled `problem`, and `tools/inbox.py` lists it as PROBLEM WITH A PLACE. It is a tip like any other: a session looks at the place and its sources before changing or removing anything.
- **Numbered discs are what a first visit shows again** (November 24, 2026: "the bubbles work a little bit better for actually finding buildings"). The heat map is the choice under the gear (`heat`, now off unless chosen); a reader who had switched it either way keeps what they chose. The tests switch it on with `window.__atlasSet('heat',true)`.
- **The search box also goes to a spot** (November 23, 2026; `readCoords()`, `goSync()`, `goTo()`, `goLook()`; test in `tools/places_test.js`). The owner finds a street in Google Maps and wants to be taken to it here. Pasted coordinates, or a Google Maps link that carries them, move the map at once and put a pink ring on the spot (a tap on the ring opens the small box); they are not a search of the places, so the list stays whole. Words still narrow the list, and a row under the box offers to look them up as a street, town or address: one question to OpenStreetMap's Nominatim, sent only on that tap or on Enter. **Never make it ask as you type**: Nominatim's rules forbid search-as-you-type, and an app that does it is blocked. A longitude given without its minus is taken as west. A short link (`maps.app.goo.gl`) carries no coordinates and the box says so. Ideas not built yet (searching the leads, forgiving spelling, nearest first, showing why a result matched) are in the owner's work list.
- **The long-press box, made small** (November 23, 2026; `onContext()`, `ctxClose()`; test in `tools/spots_test.js`). The owner uses three things from it, Google Maps, Street View and Google Earth, and the box had grown tall enough to push them under a fold. Now: the coordinates are one small line that copies when tapped; "+ My places" and "Send to Atlas" sit side by side; the three maps are one row; Google satellite, Old air photos and "Whose land is it?" are under "More", which a desktop (a mouse that can hover) opens by itself and a phone leaves shut. The paragraph that said which of Add and Send is which is gone from the box: each button's own hint says it. "Measure drive times from here" is gone from the box too; the start place is chosen from the card and the picker on the Places tab. On a map too short to hold the box above the point (the layer bar dragged right down) the box sits flat against the top of the map (`.ctxflat`) with a ring on the spot, where before it turned into a scrolling bubble. Tested with a mouse; the long press itself and the feel of the small buttons under a finger are the owner's to say.
- **Spots through the form** (November 21, 2026; `spotCard()`, `tools/spotlines.py`, `tools/inbox.py`; tests: `tools/spots_test.js`, `tools/spotlines_test.py`). The spots card's first button sends the spots as one submission of the Google Form: Place "Spots: N marked for research", and one numbered line a spot (point, then the few words) in the text, 1,400 characters a link, so a long list goes as several. `inbox.py` prints each spot as its own lead with what the Atlas has within 300 m. "Copy for Claude" still works for the owner.
- **Pins and the numbered discs** (November 21, 2026; `syncMarkers()`, `clusSize()`, `.pin.fav.far`; test: `tools/places_test.js`). While discs are showing, a favourite keeps its own pin but drawn smaller and under the discs, and any pin that stands alone and would sit on a disc is drawn on the disc's rim, over it, on the side its place is on (only where it is drawn moves, not the place): in the middle a pin hid the number, and pushed clear of the disc it was not plain which disc it went with.
- **The ring on a "point me there" target** is an HTML marker (`.nmring`), not a shape in the drawing layer: a shape there is stretched with the map during a pinch zoom (test: `tools/near_test.js`).
- **Hours on a place page** (November 19, 2026; test: `tools/places_test.js`). `hoursDd(s)` builds the Hours row in "Getting there"; `takesVisitors(s)` decides who gets the look-up link; `hoursAsOf(s)` finds the month from the place's batch. The link is a Google Maps search by name, town and state, not by coordinate: a coordinate search drops a pin and shows no business hours. The episode kit's "Before you go" lines end with the recorded hours. Hours are plain text on purpose; nothing in the app reads them as times, so there is no "open now" (section 4, "Access and hours").
- **The Places tab's header is one row**: the search box, a Filters button and "+ Add" (`app.body.html`, `buildFilters()`, `syncFcount()`; test: `tools/places_test.js`). Filters opens the panel under it; the button fills with the accent colour and shows a number while any filter is on, and "Show N places" at the foot of the panel shuts it. The shortcut chips (Newest, Ruins you can walk to, Within 30 min and so on) sit at the top of that panel, not above the list. A chip that is on is filled with the accent colour and ticked; the earlier pale tint could not be told from off on a dark screen. The start place for drive times is chosen only under the gear (Settings, "Start place"); a place page shows one drive time, from that start, with a "Change start place" button. Until the reader chooses, the start follows the start city nearest to where they are (`nearHome()`). **"Nearest first" counts from where the reader is while their location is switched on** (`nearFrom()`, `dtl()`, `nearMoved()`), and from the start place otherwise; the sort box names which ("Nearest to you", "Nearest to Boston"). From a live position the times are straight-line estimates marked `~`, the position is re-read only after a quarter of a mile so the list does not reshuffle while it is being scrolled, the "Drive time" filter counts from the same origin, a place page shows the time from where you are above the time from the start place, and its Google Maps directions leave the origin out so they start from the phone.
- **Speed.** Measure before and after with `node tools/perf.js` (start-up, typing in the search box) and `node tools/drag.js` (a drag of the map, zoom steps); both slow the CPU four times to stand in for a mid-range phone. What the November 2026 pass found and did: a drag was already smooth; a zoom step took about half a second, four fifths of it Leaflet working out all 108,000 points of the old rail and canal lines again, so a piece of line is now on the map only while its half-degree cell is near the view (`syncLines()`), a thinned copy is drawn at zoom 9, and at zoom 8 and under each kind of line is one thinned layer (`lineWide()`); one layer draws casing and colour (`CasedLine`). Pins that crowd together when zoomed out to zoom 9 or wider become numbered discs (`clusterPlan()`; a switch in Settings, on by default); a pin's icon is rebuilt only when it would look different. The list shows its first 120 rows and a button for the rest. Start-up went from 2.1 s to 1.1 s on that slowed profile and a zoom step from about 480 ms to 190 ms.
- **What is kept on the map** (second pass, November 17, 2026; test: `tools/overlay_test.js`; measure with `tools/zoom_prof.js`). The rule behind all of it: Leaflet works out the position of every layer on the map again at each zoom step, on screen or not, and every layer costs something even when it draws nothing. With everything switched on the map held about 6,100 layers, and a zoom step held up a slowed phone-size browser for about a quarter of a second. Now it holds 150 to 250. (1) **Pins**: only the places in view and a screen's width beyond each edge have a pin on the map (`syncMarkers()`, called when the map stops moving; `pinPool` keeps the pins already made). All 800 on the map cost about 80 ms a step where no pins cost 16. The chosen place always keeps its pin. Grouped pins are made again only when the groups change. (2) **Lead dots, vanished places, mines and pocket discs** go through `viewDots()` and `viewSync()`: only those in or near the view are on the map, and a dot that stays in view is kept, not made again. Pocket discs must be drawn plain pockets first and the best town centres last, so `pockZoom()` puts the order right (`bringToFront`) whenever a disc joins ones already drawn. (3) **Old lines** have five levels (`LLEV`, `syncLines()`): zoom 13 and closer, one layer for each piece of a line at full detail, as before; wider than that, one layer for each kind of line in each square of the map, thinned to about a pixel, with squares of half a degree at zooms 11 and 12, one degree at 9 and 10, two degrees at 7 and 8 and eight degrees at 6 and wider. Zoom 9 went from 337 layers to 35. The wide levels are drawn with square ends and plain corners (`lineStyle(k,true)`): rounding them was a third of the drawing cost and cannot be seen on a line under 3 pixels thick. The white casing stayed: without it the lines are hard to read on satellite. Adding a new overlay of dots: use `viewDots()`; do not put a whole data set on the map. Lead dots also have a floor, zoom 6 (`LEADZ`), since the leads came to cover the continent on November 19, 2026 (section 8, "Leads for every state and province").
- **How the old lines load** (`LX`, `lineStatesIn()`, `lineFetch()`, `lineWideFetch()`, `lineForget()`, `linesWarm()` in `app.js`; `tools/lines_split.py`; the lines store in `sw.js`; test: `tools/lines_test.js`). The Atlas holds lines for nine states, 6 MB in all, and a reader looks at one or two, so since November 17, 2026 the hosted page no longer fetches one file. (1) `make.py` cuts `build/lines.json` into a file for each state, a thinned picture of them all (`wide.json`) and an index that goes in the page data as `lx`: for each state a stamp of its file and the quarter-degree squares its lines touch. (Half-degree squares went out first and were too coarse: a line filed under New York dips a few kilometres into Pennsylvania near Great Bend, which put New York in a square 55 km deep, and a phone looking at Scranton fetched all of New York for nothing. Seen on the published site the same evening and changed.) (2) From zoom 9 the app fetches every state with a square in or near the view. At zoom 5 to 8 it draws from `wide.json`, fetched only when the view meets the box round all the lines. Wider than zoom 5 no lines are drawn, in any copy: with the whole country in view they are a smudge on one corner. (3) A place page fetches the state it is in whatever the switches say, because it lists the old lines passing the place; "Save for offline" does the same for its places. (4) `LINES` is whatever has arrived so far, so nothing may assume a line is there. A tap at a wide zoom on a line whose state has not been fetched opens a short box with "Zoom in here" in place of the line's own box. When a file arrives, `lineForget()` takes what was drawn from the old set off the map and `syncLines()` builds it again for the view. (5) Each address carries its file's stamp (`data/lines/ny.json?v=6b965d7f`), and the offline worker keeps these files in a store of their own, `atlas-lines`, which a new build does not empty: a state is downloaded again only when its lines change, where the one file used to be downloaded again at every build. (6) The Android app and the single-file copy still read every line from one file (`LX` is null there), so `data/lines.json` must stay a complete list under that name: apps already installed fetch it. Over the wire (gzip): New York 494 KB, Pennsylvania 707 KB, the other seven states 15 to 130 KB each, the wide picture 278 KB, the one file 1.7 MB (it was 0.7 MB).
- **Search** (`hay()`, `qTier()`): each place's words are gathered once, lower-cased with accents removed, from its names, town, years, kind, what is left, region, texts, access note and series tags. Every word typed must be found somewhere; places with all the words in the name list first, then name or town, then the rest, each group in the chosen sort order.
- **A phone on its side** (the last block of `app.css`): the menu on the left (`--sidew`, 270 to 360 points), the map on the right, the layer bar across the top of the map, and Map options and Settings opening over the menu so the map stays in sight. The rule goes by the shape of the window (wider than tall, at least 560 wide, at most 540 tall), so it covers phones on both sides of the old 820-point switch and leaves an upright phone with the keyboard up alone. Why: upright rules on a sideways phone left the list in the last 95 points along the bottom edge, where Android takes a swipe as "home" or "switch app" (the owner hit this while filming). Nothing that scrolls may sit along the bottom edge in that layout. A short, wide desktop window gets it too. After changing the layout run `tools/land_test.js`.
- **Trip clock** (`tripClock()`, `sunTimes()`): the Trip tab takes a day, a leaving time and a time at each stop (a setting, `tripStop`) and shows when each stop is reached, when the day ends, and sunset at the last stop, with a warning from the first stop that runs past it. Legs use rough estimates until "Get real drive time" has asked the routing server, which also returns each leg. Sunset is the NOAA sunrise equation, good to a couple of minutes.
- **Where a trip starts and ends** (November 21, 2026; `tripFrom()`, `gmapsLinks()`; test: `tools/trip_test.js`). The route handed to Google Maps names no start: Google then begins at the phone's own position and offers Start (a route that began at fixed coordinates only offered a preview). Nothing goes back anywhere: the round-trip tick and "Back at ..." are gone, because the way home is one tap in Google Maps. The Atlas's own estimate begins at the reader's position while the blue dot is on, otherwise at the start place if one was chosen, otherwise it is between the stops only, and says which; the Trip tab no longer asks for a start place. Ten stops go in one link.
- **Save this map view for offline** (`viewBox()`, at the foot of Map options): every leaf-off, LiDAR and USGS topo tile inside the view, down to a chosen zoom, through the same `saveArea()` as a place or a trip; 4,500 tiles at most. The street map and satellite are not saved, because their owners do not allow copies to be kept; say so if asked to add them. After changing either, run `tools/trip_test.js`.
- **How the slow layers load** (`TQ`, `fetchTile()`, `tileBlob()`, `FnLayer` in `app.js`; test: `tools/tiles_test.js`). New York's aerial and elevation servers cut each tile to order, and about one request in thirty hangs for 15 seconds and is then reset (measured November 17, 2026, from the build machine). Four rules follow. (1) A tile request that has not answered in about five seconds is dropped and asked again, up to three tries, each allowed longer; the limit stretches on a slow connection. (2) Requests run eight at a time for each server, newest first, and a tile the map no longer shows is never asked for, so zooming through ten levels fetches only the level you stop on. (3) While a cut-to-order tile is on its way, the same ground is drawn from a tile the browser already holds, a wider one stretched or the four finer ones shrunk (`_ghost`), and replaced when the sharp one arrives. (4) Zoomed far out (below zoom 11 for the latest flight, below 9 for an older year or for LiDAR) the layer shows Esri's world imagery or hillshade, which are ready-cut tiles; the leaf-off layer used to be black below zoom 9. On a wide screen at ordinary sharpness (`BIGT`: longest screen side 1,200 points or more, pixel ratio 1.4 or less) these layers use 512-point tiles, a quarter as many requests; a phone keeps 256-point tiles drawn at 512 pixels. `FnLayer.saveUrl()` gives the address that will be fetched for a piece of ground, and the two "save for offline" lists use it, so a saved area matches what the map asks for on that device. Areas saved on a wide screen before this change were kept under the small-tile addresses: with no signal the map falls back to those, and "Save again" stores them the new way. Four more rules came with the states added on November 17, 2026. (5) The usual wait is kept for each server (`TQ.hav`), not once for all: the USGS elevation service and Pennsylvania's aerials take three to ten seconds a tile and start with a 15-second limit (`SLOWHOST`), and no try waits longer than 20 seconds. (6) The world's ready-cut tile is laid beneath a cut-to-order tile (`fast` on the layer, `lay()` in `createTile`) in three cases: nothing held could stand in and the state tile has not come after 1.2 seconds; the state tile came back as a PNG, which means it has holes (asked for with `format=jpgpng`); or the state server failed. So a slow or missing state shows satellite or the coarse hillshade, not a blank. (7) A big tile's own `z` is one less than the map's zoom, so `FnLayer._req` passes the map's zoom as `gz` and `leafUrl()` and `lidarUrl()` test `c.gz||c.z`. Without it a wide screen crossed each threshold a level late and asked a slow server for four small pictures in place of one large one, with no stand-in (seen with 3DEP: 36 requests and 15 seconds for a view that now takes 9 requests). (8) The credit line names only the states in view (`creditFor()`, refreshed when the map stops moving): with ten states' offices on one line it wrapped to two lines on a phone.
- The same `app.js` runs inside the Android app. Anything app-only sits behind `AT` (running in the app) or `NATIVE` (the phone's recorder is available); the website must behave exactly as before when both are false. Run `tools/app_smoke.js` as well as `tools/smoke.js` after touching the field log, old maps, or data loading.
- **Near me** is the Near tab (`renderNear()`, `nmDraw()` and the other `nm…` functions in `app.js`). It lists every target within `U.ui.nm.r` miles of the walker, nearest first, and alerts when one comes within `U.ui.nm.al` metres (0 = off); `U.ui.nm.k` says which kinds are in. A target is `{id,k,la,lo,n,t,o}` built by `nmCollect()` from the places (`p:`), the OpenStreetMap leads (`l:`), vanished places (`v:`), mines (`m:`) and old town centres (`c:`, listed but never alerted). To put a new layer in the list, add it to `NMK` and `nmCollect()`, give its ids a prefix of their own, and call `nmLoaded()` when its data arrives. Every GPS fix goes through `nmFix()`. A page cannot run with the screen off, so in the Android app `nmPush()` hands the recorder everything within 25 miles (`FieldLog.targets`), and the recorder does the buzzing while a field log records (section 12). After changing any of it run `tools/near_test.js`. **What buzzes** (November 19, 2026; `nmBuzzes()`, test `tools/nothing_test.js`): never an old town centre; a place with nothing to see (`nothingToSee()`: Nothing visible or Marker only, not Under water) only when "No buzz for places with nothing to see" is off (it is on by default); a vanished place only when "Buzz for vanished places too" is on (off by default). They are still listed in the Near tab. The same rule decides the list handed to the Android recorder. "Hide places with nothing to see" (off by default, Settings, Map) takes those places off the map, the Places list and the Near tab, unless the reader picks "Nothing visible" or "Marker only" in Filters. Three things are never hidden (`goneHid()`): a kind whose chip is chosen in Filters, the place that is open, and a stop on the trip. The Places list ends with a line saying how many places the setting is hiding, with a "Show them" button that switches it off; a search that finds only hidden places shows the same line, so a hidden place does not look like a gap in the Atlas.
- **Silencing one place** (`nmMuted()`, `nmMute()`, `U.mute`, a list of Near ids). Tap a row in the Near tab and the card has "Never buzz here": that target stays in the list, marked "silenced", and never sets off the alert again, in the browser or through the phone's recorder (`nmBuzzes()` is the one gate for both). It is for the place beside the reader's own house. Settings, Near me, says how many are silenced and can let them all buzz again. Without it a place buzzes each time you come back from more than twice the alert distance away. Test: `tools/nothing_test.js`.
- **"Keep the map on me" lets go when you move the map** (`geo.fol`, `geo.auto` in `locate()`). The map follows the dot only while it has hold of it. It lets go when a move that was not its own (a drag, a tap on a place) leaves the dot at the edge of the screen or off it, and then no fix moves the map. A tap on Me takes hold again and recentres; a tap on Me while it has hold switches location off, as before. Before November 20, 2026 the next fix dragged the map back to the dot while the reader was looking at something else. Test: the last block of `tools/near_test.js`.
- **Map options and Settings, tidied November 19, 2026.** Every switch in Map options that has an explanation also gives it as a tooltip (`title`), for a pointer resting on it; "What do these do?" is a filled button so it is seen; the small key of pin shapes is gone from the sheet (the Guide tab has the whole legend). In Settings a choice sits on one line, its name left and its list right, and both sheets have a rule between sections (`app.css`, "the two sheets, tidied"). **A new reader starts with every overlay off**, old rail lines and canals included (`U0`): they are a tool for hunting leads, not the first thing to see. Readers who already had them on keep them. Grouped pins are coloured by count, cool to hot, in five steps (under 10, 30, 100, 300, and above; `.clus.c0` to `.c4`), and the two biggest steps are a little larger. Tests: `tools/settings_test.js`, `tools/overlay_test.js`.
- **The episode kit** (the Episode kit button on a finished field log; the `kit…` functions in `app.js`) turns one walk into what a video needs. `kitStops()` finds the Atlas places the track came within reach of (60 m for an exact pin, the pin's own precision otherwise, at most 250 m) and takes the longest visit to each as the stop. `kitParts()` cuts the walk into walking and stops and files each clip, photo and note under one. `kitChapters()` writes draft YouTube chapters, counted as if the clips were joined end to end uncut; with fewer than three stops the notes become chapters too. `kitDescription()` writes the description from each place's `hook`, `accn` and `links`, so a place record with good sources gives a good description. `kitMap()` draws the 1920 × 1080 route picture on the USGS base map (public domain, which is why it is used: do not swap in a commercial base map). The picture and the distance use `trimmed()`, the same privacy trim as a shared walk. After changing it run `tools/kit_test.js`.
- Installed apps keep their copy of `app.js` until the owner installs a new build, but they fetch new place data straight away. So place data must stay readable by older app code: **add fields, never rename or remove one the app reads.**

## 10. Known gaps

**Things left from when the Atlas was one person's map of the country round Albany** (the owner, November 21, 2026): the Atlas is now a map of the whole United States and Canada, and anything in the code or the app's words that assumes Albany or one reader's home (a default city, an example, a label, a "from Albany") may be taken out by the maintainer when met, without asking first. Taken out so far (November 21): the special opening view for an Albany start place, the Capital Region wording in the permission letter, the New York and Adirondack wording in the Guide tab's how-to and safety lists, and the local street names in the "five tells". The Guide tab's "Video series ideas" and the "Video series" filter went the same day (the owner: he picks his own); places keep their series tags and `check.py` still reads the allowed tags from the list in `make.py`. The research toolkit stays, Capital District rows and all, as a list of resources that may grow.

**Asked for and not built yet** (the owner, November 20, 2026). (1) *Visit pins that belong to a place*: a field log started from a place page keeps its notes and photo pins with that place; they stay off the main map and show under "From visits" when the place is open; a log started anywhere else behaves as now. (2) *Sharing them*: start with notes and positions through the existing send button, and a link from a pin to the moment in the owner's video. (3) *Photos*: hosted in a storage bucket (Cloudflare R2 or Backblaze B2 were the two talked about; prices and free limits not yet checked), with the Atlas holding only links; the same bucket could take readers' photos through a small form with no account, with the owner approving each one. His own PC is behind Starlink's shared address and is not the place to host them.

**Since November 18, 2026 a research session does not add lines here.** Each batch has its own note, `notes/<batch key>.md` (what the batch is, what was not checked, its leads, its tips), and `tools/start.py` prints the notes that bear on a piece of ground. The lines below are the batches before that date and the gaps in the tools.

- Red brick (November 18, 2026). 50 places were marked from their own Atlas text, where the text says brick (`tools/bricks_text.py`): nobody looked at a picture for those, and brick was taken as red unless the text says otherwise. About 610 standing places, ruins and traces are not looked at yet; `python3 tools/brick.py` counts them and the "brick" type of session in `PROMPTS.md` works through them a picture at a time. The picture route was tried on two places from the build machine and worked; Wikimedia's summary service answers 429 there, so the tool reads the article as raw text and fetches its first picture by file name, waiting when refused.
- What is wanted, and the age filter (November 18, 2026). The day's first wording told sessions that red brick is what the channel looks for most and to put it first; the owner corrected that the same evening, and sessions started in between may have leaned toward brick. Red brick is now recorded and filterable, nothing more. The "Dates from" filter and the "Standing, from before 1800" shortcut read the earliest year in a place's `years` text: 109 of 803 places have no year it can read (38 have none at all) and are left out when the filter is on.
- Research that merges by itself, the one-word start and `CONTRIBUTING.md` (November 18, 2026). Not tried by anyone outside: no stranger has yet followed `CONTRIBUTING.md`, and the claim step of `choose.py` cannot reach the owner's repository from a copy, so two outside sessions can draw the same ground (the open pull requests they read are the owner's, which catches most of it). `choose.py` now also names old town centres with no Atlas place; that list exists only for the seven states the historic-pockets layer covers.
- Old lines, for later: a railway mapped only as a route relation is mostly left out (section 8). About 1,700 km in New York and 800 km in Pennsylvania; taking them needs a cut against the railroads in use.

- Most pins added in November 2026 for New York and Massachusetts were not checked against imagery; their pin notes say so. Rhode Island pins were each looked at once on the state aerial; in the first pass none was moved, and in the second two were moved (Butts Hill Fort, Miantonomi tower) and one was downgraded (Nine Men's Misery, whose article coordinate falls on a marsh).
- Northwest Connecticut (Litchfield County) had its first real pass on November 17, 2026 (`data_ctnw.py`, 15 places, by an Opus session working from PROMPTS.md). Its pins were looked at on the Esri satellite, where leaves may be on, except Sharon, which fell inside the box the pin check uses for New York's leaf-off aerial: that worked by luck. Connecticut publishes its own aerials; nobody has checked whether they are leaf-off or added them. Many Connecticut park pages have moved to ctparks.com and some old addresses return "not found" (Camp Columbia, Humaston Brook); DEEP's "Other State Parks and Forests" page covers the undeveloped parks. Wikipedia's coordinate for Burr Pond State Park is in the middle of the pond. That session did not update this guide; the table row and this paragraph were added afterwards.
- Huntingdon County, Pennsylvania (`data_pac_eclm.py`, November 17, 2026, 15 places, by an Opus session working from PROMPTS.md, region `pac` drawn with `choose.py`; Etna Furnace is just over the line in Blair County). Sources: Wikipedia read with `wiki_raw.py`, the DCNR park pages and the National Park Service's Mt. Etna page, Penn State's Stone Valley Forest brochure and public-use page, the Friends of the East Broad Top museum page and HMdb marker texts, all read with `page.py`. Every National Register form for the county on npgallery came back as a one-page scan with no text layer, so none was read and the Register details are Wikipedia's summaries. The East Broad Top's own site refused `page.py` (HTTP 403), so train days and tour times were not checked. Not checked on the ground: what still stands at Huntingdon Furnace, Barree, Juniata Iron Works and Pennsylvania Furnace (all private, look-only or ask-first); whether St. Mary's covered bridge and Corbin Bridge are open; the Minersville pin sits on a LiDAR bench read as an oven bank, and where the game-land line runs through the ovens is not known; the Greenwood stacks and Paradise stack are under trees on the aerial. Penn State's Stone Valley Forest (Monroe Furnace) is open to the public but is not in the federal public-land map, so the pin check says CHECK ACCESS there. The Raystown Branch guard lock went to LEADS.md: its Register coordinate is in a ploughed field and no public way to it was found.
- Hunterdon County, New Jersey (`data_njc_mpsa.py`, November 17, 2026, 13 places, by an Opus session working from PROMPTS.md, region drawn with `choose.py`): almost all standing places, since Hunterdon's iron furnaces are under Spruce Run Reservoir or on unknown ground (see `LEADS.md`). Facts come from HMdb.org marker texts (read in full with `page.py --all`), Wikipedia, and museum pages; markers written by the Union Forge Heritage Association carry most of the High Bridge history and are one local source. The New Jersey State Park Service pages (`nj.gov/dep/parksandforests/...`) answer `page.py` with an empty page and the web fetch tool with a bare title, so their hours (grounds sunrise to sunset, no fee) were read only as quoted in web search results; `dandrcanal.org` gave the canal park's hours and dog rule. The High Bridge borough parks page answered 403 and Hunterdon County's park pages were not found, so borough and county hours were not checked. Not checked: whether Solitude House opens at all since its museum closed in 2012, what is left of the Lambertville outlet lock under the trees, which TISCO building is which, ownership of the two churches, and where to pull off at Green Sergeant's bridge. Pins were looked at on the Esri satellite and the LiDAR; the High Bridge arch pin was moved onto the stream crossing under the embankment, and two parking points were moved onto lots.
- Amsterdam, New York and the country round it (`data_amsterdam.py`, November 17, 2026, 10 places, by an Opus session working from PROMPTS.md): the mill dams and the sealed Mohasco Power House on the North Chuctanunda Creek, Yankee Hill Lock 28 and Empire Lock 29 at Schoharie Crossing, Guy Park Manor, Old Fort Johnson, the Queen Anne Parsonage, Wolf Hollow Road (from `LEADS.md`) and a farm foundation in the Schenectady County Forest at Duanesburg. Wikipedia was read as raw text; the state parks page, county and land-conservancy pages, HMdb marker pages and news pages through `page.py`; the county's printed creek tour (`Touring the North Chuctanunda Creek`) as text from its PDF. That tour has no printed date (the county posted it in 2019), so the trestle footings, coal heaps, oil tank and mill buildings it describes were not confirmed as still there. Not checked: anything on the ground; whether Guy Park's restoration is finished or the house open (the state aerial shows a work yard round it); the 2026 season at Old Fort Johnson (its page gave 2025 dates); hours for Wolf Hollow and the Chuctanunda Creek Trail (the city's trail page, `amsterdamny.gov/Chucktrail`, named on the trail markers, returned "not found"); who owns the Queen Anne Parsonage, whose pin is left on Queen Anne Road beside the marker. Every pin was looked at with `pincheck.py --lidar`: none sits on a house or a yard, none marks only a trailhead, and the Wiggins foundation shows on LiDAR as a small square hollow under pines. The Amsterdam city pins are `view`: the creek banks, mills and power house are city or private property. `LEADS.md` has four more from this pass (Harrower Pond Dam, the Hagaman mill dam, Chalmers Knitting Mills, the Schoharie Creek Preserve barns).
- The country round Syracuse (`data_syracuse.py`, November 17, 2026, 11 places, by an Opus session working from PROMPTS.md): the Butternut Creek and Limestone Creek aqueducts and the Fayetteville lime kilns on the Old Erie Canal, Clinton's Ditch at Camillus, Lock 51 and the Jordan Aqueduct, the mill ruins on Skaneateles Creek (Charlie Major Nature Trail), the Mud Lock and Guard Lock 2 of the old Oswego Canal, the Fort Brewerton earthworks and the vanished Long Branch amusement park. Sources were read through `wiki_raw.py` and `page.py`: Wikipedia, eriecanal.org, HMdb and Pomeroy Foundation marker pages, the state parks page, Onondaga County Parks, the towns of Skaneateles and Volney, and a draft Old Erie Canal waterfront plan. Every pin was looked at on the leaf-off aerial and the LiDAR; Lock 51 and the Mud Lock were moved onto chambers seen there, and the LiDAR shows the whole star of Fort Brewerton. Not checked: the Fayetteville kilns themselves (the pin is an information board), which ruin stands where on the Charlie Major trail (the pin is a trail bridge), the hours of Camillus Erie Canal Park (its site refused the connection), Jordan Canal Park and Lock 51 Park, who owns the Fort Brewerton grounds, and the Great Bear parking lot (no trailhead point). Pratt's Falls (the first mill in the county, 1796), Gere's Lock (Lock 50, on private land) and the Rockaway resort pillars on Onondaga Lake are in LEADS.md. The Finger Lakes, Oswego city and the country south of Tully are still empty.
- Old Salem and Winston-Salem, North Carolina (`data_oldsalem.py`, November 17, 2026, 12 places, by an Opus session working from PROMPTS.md), the first real pass in North Carolina: six Old Salem archaeology sites (the Reich-Hege cellar hole, the Tavern smokehouse foundation, the Second House outline, the Builders' House site, the St. Philips graveyards, the line of the 1820 mill race), Bethabara's stabilized cellars and fort, God's Acre and mill site, Lake Katharine at Reynolda, Idols Dam on the Yadkin and the flooded Piedmont Quarry. The Old Salem entries rest almost wholly on Old Salem's own marker texts as transcribed on HMdb; Bethabara on the 1998 landmark nomination (text layer read) and the park's pages (historicbethabara.org answers 403 to scripts; the web fetch tool read it). Not checked: anything on the ground; whether the Idols station building still stands; when Lake Katharine became a wetland; the exact spots of God's Acre, the Bethabara mill and the Reynolda boathouse (all `area`); parking for the Old Salem entries (OpenStreetMap maps no lot at Old Salem's visitor center, so they have no `th=`). Pins were looked at on Esri satellite and the LiDAR sheet; one was moved (Piedmont Quarry, onto the overlook pier). North Carolina has no leads layer, no rail lines and no law note.
- Washington County, New York (`data_washco.py`, November 17, 2026, 12 places, by an Opus session working from PROMPTS.md with PLACE and TYPE left to it; chosen because region `wash` held 4 places against 13 each in Saratoga and Rensselaer): the old Champlain Canal's junction lock and Lock 15 at Fort Edward, Lock 13 and the Moses Kill aqueduct near Fort Miller, combined Locks 16-18 at Fort Ann, the original canal walls at Whitehall, the unfinished 1837 railroad piers in the Hudson at Hudson Falls, the Old Fort House, the Old Stone House Library, Skene Manor, and the Shushan and Rexleigh covered bridges. The canal entries rest mainly on the American Canal Society's Champlain Canal index (a PDF revised in 2011, read as text: a condition note and a coordinate for every old lock) and, for the junction lock, the village's 2023 trail feasibility report (read in full as text); the rest on HMdb marker pages, Wikipedia as raw text, the village, town, museum and covered-bridge society pages through `page.py`, and one hiker's trail write-up (bikeitorhikeit.org) for Lock 15 and the Fort Ann trail. Not checked: anything on the ground; whether Lock 15 and its dry dock are still there (the trail writer had not gone in) or on village land; who owns Lock 13, the Moses Kill aqueduct and the Fort Ann and Whitehall towpaths; the 2026 hours of the Old Fort House and the library; parking at Shushan, Lock 13 and the aqueduct. Pins were looked at with `pincheck.py --lidar` on the leaf-off aerial (Lock 13 fell back to Esri satellite) and the LiDAR: the LiDAR shows the junction lock walls, Lock 15's channel, the Fort Ann chamber and the aqueduct's abutment; Shushan was moved 35 m onto the bridge; the Old Fort House was downgraded to `site` because its Wikipedia point falls among trees; the Hudson Falls piers and the Whitehall walls are `area` pins on a marker. Six places are look-only (`view`), five of them because nobody could say who owns the ground. Eight more Washington County leads are in `LEADS.md` (the Middle Falls mill ruins, Lock 14, Hartford's mill site, the Fort Ann powder house, the Whitehall D&H shops, Argyle's Kilmer mill, the Eagleville bridge, the Feeder Canal lime kilns), and the duplicate Shelving Rock and Mount Hope rows there were merged. The south of the county (Cambridge, Greenwich, Salem's village) and the slate country round Granville are still nearly empty.
- From the Washington County session (November 17, 2026): the state Canal Corporation's heritage-site list (`canals.ny.gov/wwwapps/boating/heritagesites.aspx`) returns 404; `page.py` found an Internet Archive copy but could not fetch it, and in an unattended session the web fetch tool asks the owner's approval for an archive address, which never comes, so treat archived copies as unreachable when nobody is watching. `page.py --all` prints only the first 12,000 characters unless given `-n`: a 50-page PDF needs `-n 60000`. HMdb's nearby list (section 6) holds each marker's number, title and position in its links (`<li><a href='m.asp?m=N'>Title</a> (<a href='...destination=LAT,LON...`); a few lines of Python over the raw page list them, which `page.py -g` does not.
- Washington County, second batch (`data_washco2.py`, November 17, 2026, 6 places, by a second Opus session that chose the same county independently and found pull request 48 already open when it went to push; it set its own overlapping entries aside and kept only the places the first did not add): Feeder Canal Lock 12 and the Kenyon Lumber site, the Martindale boat basin, the 1906 coal silos and the lime-kiln ruins at Shermantown Road (all from HMdb marker texts, the Feeder Canal Alliance's walking and paddling pages, and Wikipedia), the old canal and trolley bed in The Nature Conservancy's Denton Sanctuary (the Conservancy's page, read through `page.py`), and the Eagleville covered bridge (Wikipedia only). It also moved the first batch's `hudson-falls-railroad-piers` pin from the roadside marker onto the piers, which show plainly on the aerial as a row of rectangular blocks crossing the river. Not checked: anything on the ground; what is left of Lock 12, the Kenyon site, the silos and the lime kilns (all `mixed`); whether Eagleville bridge still carries cars; the owner of the Denton canal strip along Route 4 and of the Feeder Canal ground other than the lime-kiln pin, which `parcel.py` puts on a state parcel (Washington County is not in the state parcel file at all). Pins were looked at with `pincheck.py --lidar`: the Martindale pin was moved off a building onto the basin and the Denton pin onto the canal bed the LiDAR shows; three parking points from `parking.py` were not used (one on the Lock C-6 chamber, one on a town street, one in an industrial yard at Shermantown Road), a reminder to look at every TRAILHEAD tile. `LEADS.md` has four more rows from it (Fort Edward's Old Moat, Kanes Falls, Bassett's Lock and Fort Miller Aqueduct No. 2, Salem's rail yard).
- The Quabbin and North Quabbin, Massachusetts (`data_quabbin.py`, November 17, 2026, 12 places, by an Opus session given "you choose" for both place and type; chosen because region `cmass` held 3 places against 48 in Worcester and 15 in the Pioneer Valley): the Rabbit railroad bed and North Dana cellar holes at Gate 35, the Gate 52 wartime range remains, Webster Road in old Enfield, the Quabbin Park Cemetery, the North Prescott church at the Swift River Valley Historical Society and the Prescott Town House in Petersham, the Keystone Bridge at Millington, the Coldbrook Springs spa village in the Ware River watershed, the Williams Homestead ruins (Swift River Reservation), the Bear's Den and Doane's Falls mill sites, and the Ware-Hardwick covered bridge. Sources: DCR's Quabbin packets, kiosk posters, brochures, event pages and the Ware River access summary, all read with the web fetch tool because mass.gov answers `page.py` with 403; the Trustees' property pages and trail-map PDFs, the Athol Daily News and Greenfield Recorder, the historical society's site, Wachusett Greenways, the Byways page and a blog through `page.py`; Wikipedia as raw text. Not checked: anything on the ground; whether the Keystone Bridge deck is open again (DCR's access page still carried its July 2021 closure of Gate 30 while DCR's own October 2026 walk went in from Gate 29 to see it), and where Gate 29 is exactly (no `th=`); what is left at Gate 52 (DCR's 2017 packet is the only source; the pin is on LiDAR shapes that may be the structures); which of two accounts of Coldbrook Springs is right (many foundations, or only the cisterns) and where the hotel stood; which building is the Prescott Town House (Wikipedia coordinate in a meadow; the house is not open to the public, so `ask`); the exact spots of the Bear's Den and Doane's mill foundations (both `area`, pinned on the reserve and the falls); Quabbin Park Cemetery's hours; parking at the covered bridge and at Coldbrook. The church's 1837 date rests on one blog. Every pin was looked at with `pincheck.py --lidar` on the MassGIS 2023 leaf-off aerial and the LiDAR: two were moved with `pix.py` (the Ware-Hardwick bridge 33 m onto the bridge; Gate 52 65 m onto rectangular LiDAR shapes), the Williams Homestead pin went to `exact` on a square hollow the LiDAR shows, and the Prescott Town House went to `area`. The `land:` line again changed between runs for the same trailhead point (Swift River's Slab City lot); `pad.py` settled two pins. `tools/geo.py` searches New York only and reads a JSON file, so addresses elsewhere were looked up by calling the ArcGIS `findAddressCandidates` address directly. `LEADS.md` has eight more rows from this pass (Millington, the New Salem station foundation, the Prescott Peninsula, the bombing-range towers, the Prescott church at South Hadley, the two mill spots, Coldbrook's hotel, Gilbertville's mills). Greenwich (Gates 43 and 44), Hubbardston, Barre and the Athol and Orange side are still empty.
- Strafford County, New Hampshire (`data_nhl_eqhp.py`, November 17, 2026, 17 places, by an Opus session given "you choose" for both place and type; `choose.py` drew region `nhl`, which held one place): Wiswall Falls mill site, the Doe Farm cellar hole, the Foss (Swain's) mill site in the Isinglass River Conservation Reserve, the First Parish meetinghouse site on Dover Point, the Damm Garrison at the Woodman Museum, the Cocheco, Sawyer, Salmon Falls and Queensbury mills, the Dover Friends Meetinghouse, Garrison Hill Tower, the Farmington pound, the New Durham meetinghouse and pound, the Milton Town House, the New Hampshire Farm Museum, Strafford Union Academy and the Blue Job fire tower. Sources: Wikipedia read as raw text for the Register listings; the Lamprey River Advisory Committee's park page and Doe Farm panels (PDF); the Town of Strafford's conservation-lands and trail-sign PDFs; the Woodman Museum's own page (hours and fees); the Dover library's tower page; one hiking blog for the Isinglass mill and cemetery. The Durham town site had moved (section 3 table), so Durham access details come from search-result text and the river committee. Not checked: hours for every place but the Woodman Museum; whether the First Parish site may be walked on; the exact spots of the Doe cellar hole, the Isinglass mill foundation (pinned at the Pig Lane bridge) and the Farmington pound, all under trees; which roof is the Friends meetinghouse; the Durham Mill Pond dam, being removed in 2026, was left out (see `LEADS.md`). Pins were looked at on the Esri satellite (leaves on) and the LiDAR; none was moved.
- Utah, the Tintic Mining District and the West Desert (`data_ut_tkty.py`, November 17, 2026, 15 places, by an Opus session given "you choose"; `choose.py` drew region `ut`, which held one place). Twelve places round Eureka, Mammoth, Silver City, Knightsville and Diamond (the Bullion Beck headframe, Main Street, the Tintic Mining Museum, the Eagle and Blue Bell mine, the Knightsville school foundation, the Tintic smelter foundations, three cemeteries, the Knight grain elevator) and the closed Tintic Standard Reduction Mill at Genola; and the Boyd, Canyon (Round) and Willow Springs Pony Express stations. Sources: the 1979 National Register forms for the Tintic Mining District, read as text from NPGallery (each `<number>_text` file is one page of a multiple-property form, so it often carries the neighbouring entries too); state markers on HMdb; Wikipedia; the NPS Pony Express pages and the BLM byway map; Eureka City's pages; and two 2024 visits (the Mining History Association field trip and the Deseret News). The Register forms are from 1977-79, and what still stands was checked only where a 2024 source or the aerial shows it. Not checked: whether the Eagle and Blue Bell headframe stands (the city's slideshow captions a picture "Collapse of Eagle Blue Bell"); the present owner of any Tintic mine ground (every Tintic pin is off mapped public land; the forms name 1979 owners); the museum's hours (the city gives none, only phone numbers for an appointment); the Division of Wildlife Resources' own pages on the Tintic mill (both 404; the closure comes from 2019 news reports); cemetery rules. Two Register-form coordinates were wrong (Eagle and Blue Bell, Knightsville school) and were not used; four pins were moved on the aerial with `pix.py`. Left in LEADS.md: the other Tintic headframes (Centennial-Eureka, Gemini, Eureka Hill, Grand Central, Beck No. 2, Yankee, Big Hill, Iron Blossom No. 3, Showers, South Iron Blossom, Eureka Lilly), the Dividend miners' dry, the Homansville lime and charcoal kilns, the Sunbeam Mine, and Fish Springs station. Tip: `wiki_raw.py` and `page.py` take `-g pattern` only as the first argument; put after the titles it is read as a title and fails quietly as NO SUCH ARTICLE.
- **A session whose shell cannot reach the web** (first seen November 23, 2026, the Sitka batch, from an assistant set up outside Claude Code). Some cloud machines let the shell reach only GitHub and the package registries. Then `pincheck.py`, `parking.py`, `nom.py`, `geo.py`, `photo_find.py` and the download of the test browser all fail, and `start.py` cannot find a town by name (give it a latitude and longitude). Test first: `curl -s -o /dev/null -w '%{http_code}' https://en.wikipedia.org/`. Such a session can still do honest work the way that batch did: read every page with its web fetch tool, look at each pin on the Esri picture at the export address in `tools/ortho.py`, and say in the pull request which tools did not run. Its places come without parking points, a public-land line or pictures; the pictures job finds pictures for them later. The lasting cure is the machine's network setting, which only the owner can change (he did, on November 23, for the map, imagery, Wikipedia and Commons hosts; ordinary web sites are still refused from the shell there, because every town has different ones, so official pages and National Register forms are read with the web fetch tool, which is the expected way and not a fault to report). The phone-size smoke test cannot run on such a machine either, because the test browser cannot be downloaded: that is not a failure. Say in one line that it did not run; GitHub's check runs it on every pull request before the merge.
- **The front page's first picture shows both stand-ins and keeps itself up to date** (November 25, 2026; `tools/shots.js` picture `01-map`, `tools/first_picture.py`, test `tools/first_picture_test.py`). The owner likes the numbered bubbles for showing how many places there are and the heat map as the other choice, so the first picture is one map with the heat map on the left of a slanted line and the bubbles on the right, each labelled. The site build retakes that one picture, and brings the caption's "More than N old places" up to date, when the map has passed another hundred places since the last one (the count is in `docs/features/01-map.count`); the other eleven pictures are still retaken by hand with `tools/shots.js` after a change to how a screen looks. If the picture cannot be taken the build goes on without it.
- **The article-photo lookup runs with every site build** (November 26, 2026; `tools/photos.py`, `ci_build.sh`, `ATLAS_PHOTOS` in `site.yml`). `photos.py` asks Wikipedia which picture each place's article carries and writes `src/photos.json`; it was a tool somebody had to remember to run "every week or two". By November 26 it had not run for six days, 3,500 places had never been asked about, and the pictures job, which takes a place only after `photos.py` has been through it, reported 11 places waiting when 4,360 had no picture. It was run in full that day (2,676 places known before, 6,187 after; 4,726 with a picture), and the site build now runs it for up to 300 new places each time, five minutes at most, and carries the file back into `source/src/`. If it cannot finish, the build goes on and the next one asks again. A pull request's check does not run it.
- **Why Missouri kept going wrong, and the rule that came of it** (November 27, 2026). Missouri is the one state that was opened for a single city: region `kcmo`, "Kansas City, Missouri", was made on November 23 for a researcher there, and the state had no other region until `mo`, "Missouri outside Kansas City", on November 25. Everything else followed from that and from the city's lying on a state line. Sessions filed St. Louis, Hannibal and the capitol under Kansas City, since it was the only Missouri region there was (66 places, refiled November 27). An hourly task tied to the city wore out the counties round it while the rest of the state stood empty. "Kansas City" is in two states and "Kansas" is inside its name, so one open pull request once took all three regions and locked every session out; Jackson County is in both states, and a dry mark went on the wrong one. And Missouri had moved its Register forms, so its listings looked unreadable. None of it was a fault in how regions are drawn: a region is only a label a session gives a place, with no boundary behind it. So a region named for one city now has one: `CITY_REG` in `tools/check.py` stops the build on a `kcmo` place more than 60 km from Kansas City and names the region to use. A second city region, if one is ever made, goes in that table with its own distance.
- **A dead address is not the end: the form finder** (November 26, 2026; `tools/form.py`, test `tools/form_test.py`). A night of Kansas City sessions called Jackson County's 65 remaining listings "no source": the Park Service's form for each is a one-page placeholder ("not yet digitized"), Missouri's copies answered 404, the web fetch tool will not open web.archive.org, and an unattended run cannot answer a fetch's approval prompt. All three roads had only been tried at their first step. What was there all along: (1) the Park Service's **record page** (`npgallery.nps.gov/AssetDetail/NRIS/<number>`) answers even when the form is a placeholder and gives the name, street address, architect, style, significant years and day of listing, which is a source; (2) **Missouri moved every form in 2025**: the state's index (`mostateparks.com/page/missouri-national-register-listings`, five county pages) links each listing to `/media/pdf/<name>`, whose viewer page holds the real file under `/sites/g/files/zuston361/files/media/pdf/2025/02/`, and beside each link stands the address, the day of listing and, where it applies, "demolished 2000; delisted". Of Missouri's 1,806 listings the Atlas lacked that day, 1,556 were in that index by name, 56 of Jackson County's 65. `form.py <number>` walks those roads in order, keeps a form's text in `research/forms/<number>.txt`, reads a scan by OCR where the machine has tesseract, and says exactly what it read; `form.py <state> "<name>"` finds the number. A listing of the last ten years often has no Park Service record; the state's copy is the road for those. Only Missouri's own copies are wired in: add another state's in `state_copy()` when a session finds where it keeps them (Kansas: `kansashistory.gov`, link names in the page's HTML). `page.py` now prints WHAT TO TRY NEXT whenever a page will not open (`form.py --ladder`), says when an old Missouri address is one that moved, no longer tells a session to open an archived copy with the web fetch tool, and says when its matching sentences were cut short. **A listing is set aside once, for everyone**: `choose.py --skip <clone> <state> <number> gone|nosource|notwanted|onatlas "<why>"` writes one line on the `claims` branch (`skip mo:85000942 <time> <tag> gone <why>`), and `gaps.py` leaves it out for a year; listings Missouri's index marks demolished or delisted leave the count by themselves. `choose.py --dry` now wants the state key (a mark for Missouri's Jackson County went on Kansas's), and when it will not mark a county with plenty left, `--leave` moves the session on with nothing marked. A listing is not proof a building stands: look at the pin and search the news.
- **What is left is counted before a session starts, and used-up ground is marked dry** (November 26, 2026; `tools/gaps.py`, `tools/dry.py`, `choose.py --dry`, test `tools/choose_test.py`). The hourly Kansas City sessions kept returning to the counties round the city after ten notes in a row had called them worked out; one spent 25 minutes and added two places. Three causes: nothing recorded that ground was used up (a tip in a note reaches the next session only after the tidy job), the draw went by how few places a region has and not by what is left to add, and each session measured the ground afresh with a script of its own. Now `tools/gaps.py <state> ["<County>"]` counts, county by county, the National Register listings with no Atlas place within 300 m and none of the same name within 3 km (archaeological sites left out), and `choose.py --claim` prints that for the state it drew: the counties with eight or more left, by lot, or nearest first when the task gives `--near <lat>,<lon>`. A session that finds fewer than eight readable sources in its first ten minutes runs `choose.py --dry <clone> "<County>" "<why>"`: one line on the `claims` branch (`dry mo:cass <time> <tag> <left> <why>`), which keeps that county out of every session's table for thirty days. The fail-safes: a county with 25 or more listings left cannot be marked (the trouble there is the sources); a session may give ground up twice, and the third time is told to publish what it has or stop and say so; a mark lapses by itself; and the owner or the maintainer takes one back with `choose.py --wet <clone> mo:cass`. The count is a guide, not the truth: Register points can be hundreds of metres off, names differ ("Long, R. A., House" is Corinthian Hall), and many listings are plain apartment blocks, so a county's number is an upper limit. It says nothing about ground with other sources, and nothing for Canada. The measure showed at once that the trouble was the task's ground and not the states: Missouri had about 1,500 listings left (St. Louis 600) and Kansas 950 (Wichita and Topeka 100 each), with next to none in the ring round Kansas City.
- **An open pull request takes its own region, not its neighbours** (November 25, 2026; `tools/claims_pr.py`, test `tools/choose_test.py`). `choose.py` counts ground named in an open pull request as taken, and used to go by any long word of a region's label, state names included: one open pull request for rural Kansas took `ks`, `kcmo` ("Kansas City, Missouri") and `mo` ("Missouri outside Kansas City") at once, and the hourly Kansas City research stopped with "every region is taken". Now a region is taken by its code in the branch name or in a batch key in the title, by a distinctive word of its name, by its state's name when the state has one region, or by its city and state together. That pull request was open at all because GitHub answered the automatic merge with a 502; `check.yml` now asks up to four times. A research pull request left open with green checks and a failed `merge` job is this: re-run the failed job, or tell the maintainer.
- **Missouri has a second region, `mo`** ("Missouri outside Kansas City", November 24, 2026): St. Louis, Springfield, the river towns and the Ozarks. Until then the state had only `kcmo`, and a Carnegie pass had nowhere to file a library in St. Louis. No start city was added for it, so drive times there are estimates until one is (section 8, "Drive times"). LiDAR finds outside the Kansas City counties now go to `mo` and no longer leave a TODO.
- **A link to one of Wikipedia's lists is not a place's own article** (November 24, 2026). `photo_find.py` counted a link such as "List of Carnegie libraries in Wisconsin" as the place's article and never offered a photo search for it, though the page takes no picture from a list. 389 places were being passed over that way; they are in the pictures job's queue now. `no_article()` in `photo_find.py` uses the same rule as `wikiTitle()` in `app.js` and `title_of()` in `photos.py`: change the three together.
- **Helpers share one allowance of web pages** (reported by the cloud research sessions, November 24, 2026; the figure is theirs and was not measured here): four research helpers run at once used up a shared limit of about 400 web pages an hour, and the Indiana pass stood still until it reset. Run two helpers at a time at most, and have each read the list or the article once and keep what it read, not fetch it again.
- **A pin just over a state line** (November 24, 2026). The state outlines are coarse (the Census Bureau's 1:20,000,000 file; Ohio is 98 points) and along a big river they wander: downtown East Liverpool, Ohio sits 1.1 km inside "West Virginia" by them, and `check.py` refused a researched library there. A pin up to 3 km over the line (`EDGE` in `check.py`) is now warned about and not refused; one deeper in another state is still an error. To ask before writing a place up: `python3 tools/check.py <clone> --where <lat> <lon> <region>`. Test: `tools/check_ok_test.py`. The East Liverpool Carnegie library is still a lead in `notes/carnegie_ohne_tjfb.md`, ready to be added.
- **A draft marked ready** is checked again by itself since November 23, 2026 (`ready_for_review` in `check.yml`); before that it waited for another push.
- **Two sessions on the same ground.** Before choosing a place, run `gh api repos/mrroot3r/Lost-and-Hidden-Atlas/pulls --jq '.[]|[.number,.head.ref,.title]|@tsv'` and read the titles of the open pull requests: an area that has an open one is taken, even though `start.py` (which reads `main`) cannot see it. On November 17, 2026 two sessions chose Washington County within the same hour, both named their file `data_washco.py`, their branch `washco-first-pass` and nine of their places with the same ids; the second found out only when its push was refused. Also give a branch a name nobody else is likely to pick (add the date or a word of your own).
- Vermont, Connecticut, New Hampshire, Maine, New Jersey and Pennsylvania have regions, start cities and a first handful of places each, and no law note in the app (optional, see section 8). All six now have their state's aerial (section 2); before November 17, 2026 the last five showed satellite, so pins in them from before that date were checked on satellite with the leaves on.
- North Quabbin, Massachusetts, a second batch (`data_quabbin2.py`, November 17, 2026, 3 places, by an Opus session given "you choose" for place and type). It chose the Quabbin for the same reason as pull request 53 (region `cmass` held 3 places against 48 in Worcester and 15 in the Pioneer Valley); that pull request was opened after this session's own check of the open pull requests came back empty, and it was found only when the list was read again at `publish.sh`. This session set its nine overlapping entries aside and kept the 1955 F-94 Starfire wreck in the Quabbin woods near Dana, John Smith's Hermit's Castle in Erving State Forest, and the burned mill ruins at Wheelwright on the Ware River. Sources: DCR's Erving State Forest draft resource management plan, the DCR Quabbin and Erving State Forest pages and the DVIDS article through the web fetch tool; the state's 2021 Wheelwright dam-removal certificate (read as text), the Athol Daily News, New England Trail, New England Aviation History and Mass Central Rail Trail pages through `page.py`. The web fetch tool read mass.gov addresses that had come up in a search result, but the same Quabbin page typed by hand asked for approval and was refused while nobody was watching; once a search returned that address, it opened. So search for an agency page first and fetch the address the search gives. Not checked: anything on the ground; where exactly the F-94 wreck lies (the pin is OpenStreetMap's point; the leaf-off aerial shows only conifers and the LiDAR a faint dimple); whether the Hermit's Castle has a new interpretive panel (DCR's plan says the old one is gone) and whether John Smith left in 1899 or 1900 (`mixed`); what is left of the Wheelwright mill after the 2025-2026 dam removal (`mixed`, look-only, private). Every pin was looked at with `pincheck.py --lidar`, and `parcel.py` put the Quabbin and Erving pins on state land and the mill on a private lot. The pin check's `land:` line again changed between runs for the same points, as the third Washington County batch found. Offered to pull request 53, not added here: on LiDAR a small sharp-edged rectangular hollow about 25 m down Webster Road on its east side (42.30578, -72.32538), where a Quabbin history blog puts the Quaint Quabbin foundation, and a gravel pull-off on Route 122 at Gate 30 (42.53467, -72.30198), which the Athol Daily News calls the Keystone Bridge's parking area. `LEADS.md` has seven rows from this session (the Petersham poor farm, Harvard Forest's "Ghosts in the Forest" sites, the South Royalston stone mill, Pynchon's grist mill, Greenwich Village at Gate 43, North Prescott at Gate 20 and the Royalston Falls pleasure ground).
- The "Ruins in the woods" batch (November 2026) has no trailhead coordinates: access notes name the road or lot. Most of its pins sit under tree cover and could not be confirmed on an aerial; `tools/pincheck.py` had no leaf-off imagery outside New York. Its Pennsylvania and Maine entries were written from one Wikipedia article each.
- The Adirondacks have 20 places. The six added in `data_adk2.py` are ruins reached on foot, and each woodland pin was looked at on LiDAR and the leaf-off aerial as well as on the pin sheet. Wikipedia covers few trail-side ruins; the state's land pages (`https://dec.ny.gov/places/<unit>`, slugs are in the site map) give trails, parking and fees but almost no history, so each further place needs a third source. `LEADS.md` lists nine more that were started.
- The LiDAR tools (section 13) work in New York and, since November 16, 2026, Massachusetts, and are tuned for 1:62,500 sheets and cellar-hole-sized pits. Massachusetts has four units swept (Dubuque, Catamount, Monroe, Savoy Mountain) and four places (South Hawley); Savoy Mountain's 47 candidates have not been looked at and Monroe's have only a first verdict; its ground model is the USGS 3DEP service, compared with the state's own copy on two chips only; its parcel rules were read from the state's use-code table and tried on points in five towns (Petersham, Windsor, Colrain, Northampton, Hawley); the 1880s Massachusetts sheets draw fewer buildings than New York's, and their first printings (1886) are pale scans the building finder cannot read, so the sweep now takes the sharpest printing from before 1916 (it was tried on the Hawley and Greylock sheets only). Mill races and charcoal hearths are not detected. Stone walls are (`tools/lidar_walls.py`, since November 2026), as unchecked lines: the finder was checked by eye on three known walled yards and five other chips and by `tools/walls_test.py` on made-up ground, not against a surveyed set of walls, so nobody knows what share of real walls it finds. On rough ground it also drew scatter: short pieces at random angles, 100 km of them in Shaker Mountain Wild Forest alone, and the first 35 units went on the map with that scatter in them for a few hours on November 16, 2026. Since then the map layer shows only pieces that form a run of 45 m, a square corner on such a run, or a yard, and leaves out whole 500 m boxes where most pieces fail (`tools/walls_structure.py`): 13,502 of 30,377 pieces, 557 km. That rule was judged by eye on ten chips in six units, not measured, and it hides some real walls (short ones standing alone, and any wall in a box of rough ground). It draws some ditch banks, old road edges and stream banks, and misses walls that are low, tumbled, shorter than 18 m or standing on a terrace edge as high as themselves. Two earlier recipes failed and are described in its header so they are not tried again: testing single cells against a median (marked road banks and chip edges), and scoring a line against the average of its two sides (let rough ground through everywhere). They have been run on 39 units. `python3 tools/lidar.py status` lists every unit swept and how many candidates nobody has looked at (353 of 594 on November 16, 2026: 306 in New York and 47 in Savoy Mountain State Forest; eight more stand at "maybe", six of them in Monroe State Forest); the record is the ledger in `source/lidar/units/`, one file per unit (LIDAR.md). An earlier version of this paragraph said 78 of the 194 candidates from the 26-unit sweep had been looked at. The true figure was 72: six were counted and never viewed. They have since been looked at, and one of them is the walled yard on High Knob. None of the forty-one `lidar` places has been visited. Twenty-one have no tax-parcel check because their county is not in the state parcel file (Schoharie, Clinton, Franklin, Fulton): Gordon Road has no ownership evidence at all, and the other twenty rest on the federal protected-areas database alone.
- Western New York (`data_wny.py` and `data_buffalo2.py`, November 2026) is 25 places: Niagara Falls, Buffalo and its near suburbs, Lockport, Fort Niagara, one Lake Ontario lighthouse, East Aurora, Springville and two in Rochester. The canal towns between Lockport and Rochester, the Genesee valley and the Southern Tier have nothing yet, and Binghamton has no places within 35 miles. Central New York (`data_cny.py`) is a first pass of 8: seven works of the old Erie and Black River canals between Boonville and Montezuma, and the Split Rock quarry; the Finger Lakes, Oswego and the country south of Syracuse are still empty. Every Western New York entry rests on a Wikipedia article and, for the state park, one parks page: opening arrangements at the Schoellkopf ruins, the Buffalo lighthouse and the Rochester aqueduct were not confirmed.
- In "More around Albany" (`data_albany2.py`) five pins are the middle of a preserve or park, not the ruin (the Hannacroix paper mill, the Peebles Island earthworks, the Wolf Creek Falls farm remains, the Cohotate ice house, the Petersburg Pass slopes), because no source gave the spot. A walk with the field log would fix each one.
- An article coordinate can be a mile off even for a famous place: Wikipedia put Canalside in Buffalo among downtown office blocks and the Kane Mountain fire tower beside a road at the foot of the mountain. Both were caught on the aerial and replaced with the mapped feature. The same happened round Buffalo with the Lockport locks (250 m off), the Buffalo Smelting Works (on the expressway) and Scoby Dam (330 m off, in woods): look at every pin.
- The vanished-places and mines layers (section 8) are raw government lists, not research: nothing in them was checked against a map, an aerial or the ground. The names file's positions for schools and churches are often the nearest road corner. Coverage differs by layer (these two cover every state, the OpenStreetMap leads every state and province, the old lines nine states, the historic pockets seven): section 8 says what each covers.
- Pins taken from OpenStreetMap through Nominatim (`tools/nom.py`) are a named mapped feature, which is usually closer than an article coordinate, but for a tunnel or a long path the point is the middle of the line.
- Rhode Island has had five passes (113 places by November 23, 2026; the fourth and fifth, `data_riprov_wssn.py` and `data_risouth_kchn.py`, took the oldest standing buildings). Every entry is a full entry: there is no "first pass" of thin entries waiting to be expanded, whatever an older note or chat may say. The third pass (`data_ri3.py`, November 17, 2026, 12 places) went after ruins and traces on foot: the Pine Top and Diamond Hill ski areas, mill ruins in town parks (Tillinghast, Valley Falls) and in Nature Conservancy, Audubon, land-trust and state preserves (Canonchet Brook, Beaver River, Parker Woodland, Hayfield, Simmons Mill Pond), the old Jamestown Bridge abutment, the Sachuest Point wartime remains, and the Seabee huts from `LEADS.md`. Wikipedia was read as raw text; agency and preserve pages in full; the ski-history sites, two hiker pages and one Providence Journal walk through a page reader asked set questions. Not checked: anything on the ground; opening hours at most places; whether the state's Jamestown Bridge shore site is open (the law says it should be, the aerial shows work going on); which Sachuest remains are left (the two sources disagree). Seven pins are `area`: no source places the Canonchet, Beaver River, Parker Woodland, Simmons, Hayfield or Sachuest remains, and Diamond Hill's lift footings were not picked out; the Canonchet pin sits on Route 3 at the trailhead's address and the Parker and Beaver River pins by houses at their trailheads, as their pin notes say. In woods the state's 2022 LiDAR (`TOPO/Elevation_2022_Lidar_web` on the RIGIS server, drawn with a Hillshade rendering rule as `lidarUrl()` does) showed foundations and races that the leaf-off aerial did not; `pincheck.py` shows only the aerial, so look at the LiDAR layer in the app as well. Before that, the second pass (`data_ri2.py`, 17 places) took Wikipedia articles through a page reader that answers set questions, not by reading the raw text, and took four places partly from the excerpts a web search returned; its header says which. Queen's Fort was nearly added twice: check `data_ri.py` for an existing id before writing a Rhode Island entry. Still no owner lookup or lot lines (the state has a tax-parcel map service at `risegis.ri.gov`; whether it carries owner names was not checked), no leads, no rebuilt rail and canal lines.
- From the third Rhode Island pass (November 17, 2026): a later pass over a state already worked goes better on a small area (one county or one management area) than on the whole state, and should try the `LEADS.md` rows in that area before hunting for new names. `tools/pincheck.py --lidar` was added after it so the pin check shows bare ground beside the aerial; it has been run on that batch only, where four pins (Canonchet Brook, Beaver River, Parker Woodland, Simmons Mill Pond) mark a trailhead or the pond and not the ruin, and Parker Woodland's ring takes in the buildings beside Audubon's Lot 1.
- From the Amsterdam session (November 17, 2026) these were added and each tried once, on one case: `nearby.py` (Amsterdam), `pix.py` (arithmetic only, not yet used to move a real pin), `wiki_raw.py --rows` (Albany County), PDF reading in `page.py` (one Register form with text, one scan), trailhead tiles in the pin check (the Amsterdam batch), the future-time check on a batch's `added`, and `setup.sh` installing missing Python modules (not run on a bare machine). In the Amsterdam batch several details, such as the 1878 date for Kellogg's dam, rest on a resident's memory quoted in the county walking-tour guide; the entries say so, and a visit or a second source should settle them.
- State imagery was added for New Jersey, Connecticut, New Hampshire, Maine, Pennsylvania and Kansas, and USGS 3DEP LiDAR for every US state without its own, on November 17, 2026 (sections 2, 8 and 9).
